> ---
> 
> ## 1. THE CHRISTIE IDEA ELIMINATION SYSTEM
> 
> **Name:** The Christie Idea Elimination (named after Agatha Christie's *And Then There Were None*)
> 
> **Job:** Solves "plausible-idea fatigue" — when AI generates 10+ content ideas that all look finished and publishable, you have no way to pick one, and the one you pick collapses mid-draft because it had no real substance underneath the polish.
> 
> **Mechanics — 4-step system, each step is a separate prompt run sequentially, with the human checkpointing between steps:**
> 
> *Step 1 — The Lineup* (standardize, don't evaluate yet)
> Prompt: "I am going to give you my current brainstormed list of content ideas. Do not generate new ones. Do not write drafts. Your job is to format every single idea into a strict 'Lineup' so I can evaluate them equally. For each idea, provide exactly and only the following: 1. The Core Thesis: One sentence stating exactly what the piece argues. 2. The Inherent Tension: What two things are in conflict? (What looks compatible but isn't? What common belief is slightly wrong?) 3. The Target Reader: Who is this for, and why would my specific reader care today? 4. The Structural Build: What exactly will I construct, show, or teach the reader to do? Do not add commentary. Format the list cleanly. Here are the ideas: [INSERT YOUR BRAINSTORM LIST]"
> Purpose: strips flashy hooks/phrasing to expose the underlying bones of each idea, forces every idea into the same evaluation format.
> 
> *Step 2 — The First Cut* (remove obvious weak suspects, fast/ruthless)
> Prompt: "Look at the Lineup we just built. We are going to execute the First Cut. Ruthlessly eliminate any idea that triggers one of these four structural red flags. State which idea you are killing and exactly which red flag killed it. 1. Too Generic: If you could generate this exact angle for any generic newsletter writer, kill it. 2. Too Thin: If the idea runs out of material at 600 words because there is no structural build to explore, kill it. (This usually happens when an idea is just a hook with no body). 3. Too Bloated: If the idea is trying to cover three distinct topics and lacks a single, focused tension, kill it. 4. Too Bloodless: If the idea is technically correct but lacks any emotional resonance or stakes, kill it. It has the personality of a parking receipt. Show me the bloodbath. Which ideas die in the First Cut?"
> Expected outcome: 2–4 survivors; if more, rerun.
> 
> *Step 3 — The Interrogation* (pressure-test survivors, AI acts as prosecutor)
> Prompt: "We now have our survivors from the First Cut. It is time for the Interrogation. Take each surviving idea and pressure-test it against these three questions. Act as a prosecuting attorney looking for structural weaknesses. 1. Where does the draft sag? Project forward to paragraph four. Does the idea lose its momentum because the premise was just a clever hook with no real substance? 2. Is it writing checks it cannot cash? Does the angle imply a massive payoff or fix that the actual structural build cannot deliver? 3. What makes this specifically mine? If the answer is 'I'll add my voice to it,' the idea fails. What specific experience, framework, or taste makes this impossible for anyone else to write? Do not be polite. Tell me exactly where each surviving idea is weakest."
> 
> *Step 4 — The Verdict* (force a single winner via strict hierarchy, not vibes)
> Prompt: "Based on the Interrogation, it is time for the Verdict. You must select exactly ONE surviving idea to become the final draft. You cannot choose based on the 'best hook'. You must make your decision based on this strict editorial hierarchy: 1. Specificity beats originality. 2. Tension is non-negotiable and always comes first. 3. Emotional pull beats hook strength. 4. Constraint beats breadth. Conflict Rules: - If you cannot have both specificity and tension, you must choose tension. Tension carries the reader. - If hook and emotional pull conflict, you must choose pull. Hooks can be rewritten later. Deliver the Verdict. Tell me: 1. Which idea survives and why it passed the hierarchy. 2. Exactly why the runner-up was executed. 3. What specific structural element the winning idea needs strengthening before I begin drafting."
> 
> **The criteria hierarchy (stated twice, used both for elimination generally and in the Verdict specifically):**
> 1. Specificity beats originality — a boring but highly specific idea always beats an interesting but vague one, because specificity gives material to build on.
> 2. Tension is non-negotiable and always comes first — needs two things that look compatible but aren't, or a widely-held belief that's slightly wrong.
> 3. Emotional pull beats hook strength — hooks can be fixed in revision; pull can't be edited in.
> 4. Hook can be taught, taste can't — between two finalists, take the one with distinct personal taste over the one with the better hook.
> 
> **The 5-category idea taxonomy used during elimination:**
> - Promising: interesting but severely underdeveloped — park in a drawer/"greenhouse," don't kill.
> - Plausible: sound decent on the surface, will never carry a full piece — most dangerous because they look finished.
> - Fragile: fall apart on one hard question — usually great hook, nothing else.
> - Bloated: trying to do three jobs at once, usually two thin ideas merged.
> - Surviving: stays compelling under pressure — specificity + tension + emotional pull + a build you can picture writing. Rare — maybe one per brainstorm.
> 
> **Inputs/outputs:** Input = raw brainstormed idea list (pasted in). Output = one surviving idea + a named weak point still to strengthen before drafting begins.
> 
> **What it can't do:** Generate taste; replace judgment if the survivor still doesn't excite you (that's data, not a fixable problem); rescue an idea you don't want to write; guarantee draft quality (idea quality ≠ writing quality). If zero ideas survive the Verdict, the instruction is: don't ask AI for more ideas — step away, find a real tension "in the wild," and build a new suspect from scratch.
> 
> **What makes it work:** (1) Strict separation of brainstorming (volume) from evaluation (precision) — never do both at once, that's what causes paralysis. (2) A pre-committed, ranked decision hierarchy that removes "vibes" as the tiebreaker, and explicit conflict-resolution rules for when two criteria contradict (tension beats specificity; pull beats hook).
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 2. THE THREE PLAYS
> 
> **Name:** The Three Plays
> 
> **Job:** Restores three distinct cognitive modes of engaging with AI (and thinking generally) that adult tool design has flattened into a single mode — "extraction" (get info, reach conclusion, produce output) — so that AI sessions stop being forgettable/shallow/predictable.
> 
> **Origin/mechanics:** Built on psychologist Mildred Parten's six childhood play stages (unoccupied, solitary, onlooker, parallel, associative, cooperative). Mia selects two that translate directly to adult thinking, and adds a third of her own:
> 
> 1. **Solitary Play → Deep Reading.** Adult translation: alone with a text, wrestling with a mind that isn't yours, productive friction of comprehension nobody can compress for you. Primary output: **rewiring** — the friction of stopping, re-reading, disagreeing in the margin is what lets the brain actually think, not just receive a conclusion. Killed by: AI summaries (give you the conclusion without the journey that makes it meaningful) and tools like Readwise that process every text identically (extract/summarize/file) with no concept that some texts deserve slow absorption, not highlighting.
> 
> 2. **Associative Play → Deep Conversation.** Adult translation: two people talking without a fixed destination, bouncing ideas, finding value in the unplanned turn/detour. Primary output: **surprise** — ideas mutate in ways neither party predicted. Killed by: agreeable AI chat — a good conversation partner changes and is changed; AI is engineered to be responsive/agreeable and doesn't carry the residue of the exchange forward, so you get exactly what you asked for (the problem). AI companion apps simulate this while stripping out the friction (disagreement/being wrong) that makes it valuable.
> 
> 3. **Dramatic Play → Deep AI Experimentation.** Adult translation: the cardboard-box-becomes-a-spaceship mode — no rules, no deliverable, zero productivity goal. Primary output: **invention** — throwing ideas at the wall, asking impossible questions, building fictional scenarios, generating twenty versions because the twenty-first (or seventeenth) has a fragment of something you didn't know existed. Killed by: adding a productivity goal, deadline, or extraction target — the instant you do, dramatic play collapses back into extraction.
> 
> **Concrete build — "How to build three Claude Projects that restore the plays":**
> - **Project 1 (Solitary Play — "reads WITH you, never FOR you"):** Instruction file tells Claude to never summarize unless asked, assume the reader wants to think harder, prioritize questions over answers. Open it when working through a dense essay/research paper or sitting with writing that moved you.
> - **Project 2 (Associative Play — "the partner who throws punches"):** Disagrees with its own calibrated perspective (defined in the instruction file). Instruction file tells Claude to surprise the user and never optimize for comfort. Open it for the feeling of a good intellectual conversation: refining a pitch, auditing competitor positioning, working a stuck decision.
> - **Project 3 (Dramatic Play — "the cardboard spaceship rebuilt"):** Generates wildly via unexpected connections, doesn't settle on one answer, encourages bad ideas as raw material for good ones. Zero judgment, zero productivity pressure. Instruction file tells Claude to never ask "what's the goal?" because having a goal is the enemy here. Open it when stuck and needing chaos, or wanting to explore without a destination.
> 
> **Reference table given in the post:**
> | | Solitary Play | Associative Play | Dramatic Play |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | Childhood mode | The Tower | The Blocks | The Spaceship |
> | Adult equivalent | Deep Reading | Deep Conversation | Deep AI Experimentation |
> | Primary output | Rewiring | Surprise | Invention |
> | Killed by | AI Summaries | Agreeable AI Chat | Productivity Goals |
> 
> **Inputs/outputs:** Input = choosing which of the three Claude Projects to open based on which mode is needed that session (not what needs to get done). Output = three distinct, non-interchangeable session types with different deliverables (understanding / an unexpected idea / raw invented material).
> 
> **What it explicitly won't do:** Won't make you more productive (productivity is what collapsed the plays in the first place); the associative-play project only approximates real conversation — a human who surprises you beats an AI calibrated to. The system requires deliberate intention each time — defaulting to the same project every morning re-collapses the plays.
> 
> **What makes it work:** (1) One Claude Project cannot hold three cognitive modes any more than one room can be a library, boxing ring, and spaceship simultaneously — so the fix is structural (three separate Projects/personalities/instruction files), not behavioral. (2) Each project's system prompt explicitly forbids the behavior that would collapse it back into extraction (no summarizing, no asking for the goal, no optimizing for comfort).
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 3. THE UNMASKING (5 hidden breakthroughs / Session Debrief)
> 
> **Name:** No single catchy name given for the overall system, but the metaphor is "the mask-pull moment" / "unmasking," styled after the Scooby-Doo reveal.
> 
> **Job:** Solves the loss of the one part of an AI session that actually compounds — the moment your own thinking shifted — because writers save the polished final output ("the costume") and let the underlying cognitive shift ("the creature") dissolve back into the chat log by the next session.
> 
> **Mechanics — a 5-layer model of what's inside every AI session, then a taxonomy of 5 breakthrough types, each with its own catch-prompt:**
> 
> *The 5 layers of a session (from most to least visible):*
> 1. The Output — the finished thing (draft, plan); decays fast because it solved one problem on one day.
> 2. The Information — raw data/facts uncovered; useful but static, doesn't change how you think.
> 3. The Insight — small perspective-shifting connections; usually forgotten the moment the browser closes.
> 4. The Thinking Move — the exact cognitive leap your brain made using the AI's material, found by scrolling back to where the conversation changed shape.
> 5. The Breakthrough — when a thinking move becomes fully reusable and compounds over time; the only true durable artifact from a chat.
> 
> *The 5 types of breakthrough ("mask-pull moments"), each with what it is / what it looks like / why it matters / the catch-prompt / what to save:*
> 
> 1. **The Reframe** — the underlying problem changes shape mid-chat; you solve a different, better problem than the one you walked in with.
>    Catch-prompt: "Did this conversation reveal that my original problem was not the real one? If yes, what problem was I actually trying to solve by the end, and what caused the shift?"
>    Save: original problem → new problem → what caused the shift.
> 
> 2. **The Accidental Connection** — a surprising lateral connection surfaces that wasn't part of the original prompt; value is the unplanned bridge, not the direct answer.
>    Catch-prompt: "What unexpected connection showed up in this conversation that I didn't ask for? Why is it more interesting than the answer I originally came for?"
>    Save: Topic A → unexpected link to Topic B → why that link matters.
> 
> 3. **The Killed Darling** — you arrive excited about a specific creative direction, then quietly abandon it mid-chat because it couldn't survive the friction of the conversation.
>    Catch-prompt: "What idea felt exciting at the start of this conversation but quietly died by the end? What killed it, and what does that tell me about what I value?"
>    Save: the idea you dropped + the reason it stopped making sense.
> 
> 4. **The Question That Cracked It** — one perfectly timed question completely alters the tone/direction of the exchange (often asked out of frustration, breaking a clinical/corporate register into something human).
>    Catch-prompt: "Which single question in this conversation changed everything? Why did that question work so well, and can I reuse it?"
>    Save: the exact question + what it opened up + where else it might apply.
> 
> 5. **The Constraint Discovery** — realizing what does NOT work becomes permanent structural architecture for future projects (a discovered rule, not a discovered idea).
>    Catch-prompt: "What did this conversation prove I should stop trying, avoid, or rule out from now on? What's the constraint, and where else does it apply?"
>    Save: the ruled-out path + the constraint it revealed + the scope of where it applies.
> 
> *The universal "Session Debrief" prompt* (used when you can't identify which of the 5 types happened, or want to catch all at once):
> "I just finished a conversation and I want to catch the breakthrough before it disappears. Review this conversation and help me find the mask-pull moment, the point where the real value appeared underneath the surface conversation. Look for these 5 types of breakthroughs: 1. THE REFRAME... 2. THE ACCIDENTAL CONNECTION... 3. THE KILLED DARLING... 4. THE QUESTION THAT CRACKED IT... 5. THE CONSTRAINT DISCOVERY... For each breakthrough you find: - Name it (which type) - Quote the exact moment it happened (the message or exchange) - Extract the reusable thinking move in one sentence - Tell me where else this might apply. If none of these happened, tell me. Some sessions are just errands. That's fine."
> 
> **Personal system for capture:** Mia keeps a single ~40-line plain text file called "thinking moves" that she adds 1–2 lines to per week — described as the least organized but most-returned-to file in her whole Obsidian vault.
> 
> **Failure modes named (honest confession, not part of the core mechanic but useful context):** "The 'I'll remember this' lie" (feel the shift, tell yourself you'll write it later, never do); "The output chase" (momentum feels good, push forward to the deliverable and ignore that the real breakthrough happened 30 messages earlier); "The 'which chat was that in?' problem" (multiple parallel sessions, can't locate which one had the insight).
> 
> **Inputs/outputs:** Input = a finished AI conversation/transcript where you felt something shift. Output = one or more named, quoted, one-sentence-distilled reusable thinking moves saved to a running log.
> 
> **What makes it work:** (1) The layered model (Output → Information → Insight → Thinking Move → Breakthrough) reorients what's worth saving — away from the artifact everyone defaults to saving, toward the cognitive shift almost nobody saves. (2) Five named, pattern-matched breakthrough types each with a single one-line prompt make the otherwise-vague act of "reflect on the session" into a fast, executable 30-second habit run only on sessions that felt different (not every session — "some sessions are just errands").
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 4. THE UNDERSTUDY (AI editor built on your own documented failure patterns)
> 
> **Name:** The Understudy
> 
> **Job:** Solves generic AI editing feedback — endless-fix spirals with no finish line, phantom problems flagged while your actual recurring crimes walk free, and erosion of self-trust — by building an editor trained specifically on your own documented writing patterns instead of generic craft advice.
> 
> **Mechanics — 3-step build process:**
> 
> *Step 1: Collect your roughest work.* Pull pieces you wrote where something felt off — heavily edited pieces you still weren't satisfied with, or rough drafts before cleanup.
> 
> *Step 2: Extraction prompt (run once against your collected work, in any LLM, author recommends Claude Opus 4.5).* Two-part prompt:
> 
> Part 1 — Pattern Analysis across four fixed categories:
> - **Word-level tells:** words appearing 5+ times across pieces; hedge words that minimize claims (just, kind of, actually, really, pretty much); zero-meaning filler phrases; repeated sentence starters ("Here's the thing," "The problem is," "What I mean is"); words that precede a claim (signaling low confidence).
> - **Structural sins:** where the main insight actually lands (buried in para 3/4/5?); is pain/problem established before the solution, or does the writer rush to answers; repeated points in different words; forced/missing/lazy transitions; overlong context-building before landing the point.
> - **Rhythm crimes:** sentence-length distribution (all medium? no variety?); paragraph-length distribution (all 3 sentences? all identical?); presence/absence of intentional rhythm (short punchy for emphasis, longer for flow) vs. flatlining; one-sentence paragraphs used for impact vs. "LinkedIn guru" tic.
> - **Emotional failures:** moments that should land emotionally but don't (jokes explained, stakes undermined, insight buried in hedging); does the writing create tension/stakes or just deliver information; is there an emotional arc or does it flatline; vague generalizations ("most people," "in a world where") instead of specifics that make readers feel seen.
> For each pattern found: name the crime, show 2–3 exact quotes as evidence, explain why it weakens the writing, note where/when it tends to appear (openings/transitions/conclusions).
> 
> Part 2 — Personal Excavation: AI asks 6 fixed questions ONE AT A TIME, waiting for an answer before the next:
> 1. "When you reread your own writing a week later, what makes you cringe? What's the thing you always find yourself fixing?"
> 2. "Is there a word or phrase you KNOW you overuse — one that friends, editors, or even you have noticed?"
> 3. "When you're tired or rushing to publish, what's the first thing that gets lazy? (Openings? Transitions? Endings? Examples?)"
> 4. "What feedback have you gotten more than once from readers or editors? Even vague feedback like 'this didn't land' or 'I got lost here' — what patterns do you remember?"
> 5. "Is there a type of sentence or move you see other writers do that you HATE? Something that makes you stop reading? (You might be doing it yourself without realizing.)"
> 6. "When a piece of yours doesn't work, where does it usually fall apart? Beginning, middle, or end?"
> After each answer, AI identifies new patterns not caught in Part 1 and folds them into the relevant category, updating/refining anything the answer contradicts.
> Final output required: one consolidated doc — all crimes organized by the four categories, the user's answers integrated (not listed separately), each crime with examples from the writing AND context from the answers, and a flag for which crimes the writer was already aware of vs. which were invisible to them. Explicitly: no generic writing advice — only patterns specific to this writer's bad habits.
> 
> *Step 3: Separate real crimes from false positives (manual review).* For each flagged pattern ask: Is this actually a crime for me, or a false positive (an intentional stylistic choice AI misread as a habit)? Is anything missing that my answers surfaced but AI didn't fully capture? What's the context — when does this happen, what triggers it? Final doc format per crime: the crime (named specifically, e.g. "buries the lead in paragraph 4," not "could improve structure"), the evidence (2–3 exact quotes from your own writing), the context (when/what triggers it).
> 
> **Step 4: Build the Claude Project.** Create a new Project ("My Editor" / "The Understudy"). Upload the consolidated crimes doc to the project's knowledge files. Set custom instructions (full text given):
> "You are my personal editor. You've watched me fail enough times to know exactly how I fail. You are NOT a generic editor. You do not give advice like 'vary sentence length' or 'be more specific' or 'consider your audience.' You catch the patterns I'm blind to. MY DOCUMENTED CRIMES: [WORD-LEVEL TELLS] [paste] [STRUCTURAL SINS] [paste] [RHYTHM CRIMES] [paste] [EMOTIONAL FAILURES] [paste] --- WHEN I GIVE YOU A DRAFT: 1. Read it ONLY looking for MY crimes listed above. Ignore generic writing issues that aren't in my crime list. I'm not asking for a full edit, I'm asking you to catch MY patterns. 2. For each instance you find, flag it with: - The crime category (e.g., 'STRUCTURAL SIN: Buried lead') - The exact quote from my draft - Why this exact instance is a problem - A suggested fix (or just 'cut this entirely') 3. At the end, give me a CRIME COUNT: how many instances of each category you found. Like a police report. 4. Be direct. Don't soften it. Don't say 'you might consider' or 'perhaps you could.' I need you to catch these crimes before my readers do."
> 
> **Sample real output shown (Mia's own crime count from running a draft through her Understudy):** Word-level: "Specific/Specifically" addiction (12+ occurrences, become a verbal tic); "Just" Leak (frequently undermines own authority). Rhythm: Numbered List Dependency (leans on lists when prose would land harder). Emotional: Stakes Announced, Not Felt (tells the reader what's at stake instead of making them feel it). Structural: none caught that pass ("suspicious"). Result: 25-minute edit instead of her usual 90-minute spiral.
> 
> **Underlying psychology cited:** "The Liking Gap" (after conversations, people underestimate how much they were liked) reframed as "The Cringe Gap" for writing — the writer scans for what felt vulnerable/embarrassing to write, not for what's actually structurally weak; the Understudy scans for what's actually weak.
> 
> **Inputs/outputs:** Input = a batch of rough/dissatisfying past drafts (for setup), then any new draft (for ongoing use). Output = a permanent, personal crimes document + a standing Claude Project that flags only that writer's documented recurring failure patterns, with quote, reason, fix, and a final crime count, on every future draft.
> 
> **What makes it work:** (1) The editor is built entirely from the writer's own evidence (their own drafts + their own self-reported failure patterns), not generic craft rules — so feedback is specific enough to actually fix instead of infinite and vague. (2) The system prompt explicitly bans generic feedback and forces a bounded, countable output ("crime count," "like a police report") that gives editing a finish line instead of an endless spiral.
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 5. THE BURNED PAGES FRAMEWORK (salvaging self-censored drafts)
> 
> **Name:** No single named framework tag in the post title itself, but internally referred to as "the Burned Pages Framework" and "Your differentiation recovery system." Structured around the Gogol *Dead Souls* burned-manuscript story.
> 
> **Job:** Solves systematic self-censorship — creators automatically delete their most differentiated, edgy, or vulnerable drafts before publishing (out of fear of alienating an audience) — by turning the deleted-draft habit into a dataset that identifies and resurrects the highest-value censored ideas.
> 
> **Mechanics — sequential multi-week system:**
> 
> *Step 1: Build the "confession booth" (2-week capture phase).* For two weeks, save every draft about to be deleted before publishing into a "Burned Manuscripts" document. For each entry, log:
> - Platform (LinkedIn, X, newsletter, blog, email, wherever it was headed)
> - Original draft (the full text nearly shipped)
> - Why I burned it (too risky / too vulnerable / too negative / too controversial / unclear / off-brand / looked stupid / similar)
> - Emotional state (anxious, embarrassed, second-guessing, worried about reactions, or a mix)
> - What I published instead (the safer version, or nothing)
> Mia's own result from this step: 11 deleted drafts in 2 weeks; 9 were critical takes on AI workflows/content frameworks killed from fear of alienating customers or seeming contrarian.
> 
> *Step 2: Pattern-recognition prompt (run against the full "Burned Manuscripts" doc plus what was published instead), in any AI model.* Prompt (paraphrased core structure given in full):
> "You are a content strategist analyzing self-censorship patterns. I'm providing deleted drafts alongside what I published instead. CONTEXT: I'm trying to identify whether my self-censorship is fear-based (removing differentiation) or quality-based (removing bad ideas). ANALYSIS FRAMEWORK: For each deleted draft: 1. IDEA EXTRACTION — core thesis (one sentence); specific supporting points that were unique/differentiated; compare to what I published instead — what differentiation was lost? 2. DELETION CLASSIFICATION — FEAR-BASED (idea was clear/well-argued but deleted due to anticipated negative reception) vs. QUALITY-BASED (idea was unclear/poorly argued/logically flawed — deletion was correct editorial judgment). 3. FEAR TAXONOMY (fear-based only) — professional risk (alienating customers, seeming unprofessional, hurting brand); social risk (looking stupid, being disagreed with, seeming contrarian); vulnerability risk (revealing failure, admitting uncertainty, exposing struggle). 4. DIFFERENTIATION ANALYSIS — what specific element made this more differentiated than what was published; was it specificity (named examples), stance (picking a side), vulnerability (admitting reality), or contrarianism (challenging consensus)? 5. STRATEGIC VALUE ASSESSMENT — would this attract the ideal audience or repel them if published; does it establish defensible positioning or just generate attention; could it become a pillar for future content or is it a one-off hot take? OUTPUT FORMAT: structured analysis prioritizing deletions by strategic resurrection value, not just differentiation."
> Mia's own result: primary self-censorship driver = fear of alienating audience by being too opinionated; deleted drafts averaged 3487 words vs. 1203 for published (drafts were being cut in half); themes: critical takes on popular content methods (5 deletions), personal vulnerability around creative failure (3), contrarian positioning against industry consensus (3); longer/more detailed arguments were more likely to be deleted than short/bland posts.
> 
> *Step 3: Build the ranked "Self-Censorship Hierarchy" using a Strategic Resurrection Score (SRS).* Prompt/formula given in full:
> "Using Step 2 analysis, build a prioritized recovery hierarchy based on strategic value. INPUT: [Paste Step 2 fear-based deletions] STRATEGIC RESURRECTION SCORE (SRS) FORMULA: Score each deletion (1–10): Differentiation (how much more unique vs. published content); Audience Alignment (attracts ideal customers=10 vs. wrong fits=1); Compounding (opens territory for 5+ follow-ups=10 vs. dead-end=1); Evidence (3+ concrete examples=10 vs. just opinion=1). SRS = (Diff × 0.3) + (Audience × 0.3) + (Compound × 0.25) + (Evidence × 0.15). OUTPUT: Ranked Deletions table (Rank | Topic | SRS | D | A | C | E | Strategic Value); Top 3 Patterns (frequency, cost/impact, fix/intervention); Top 5 Recovery Candidates each with Differentiation / Strategic value / Fear (what killed it) / Plan (Publish as-is / Revise / Add evidence) / Follow-ups (3 topics this unlocks); and a DO NOT RESURRECT list (high differentiation but low strategic value, with reasoning)."
> 
> *Step 4: Apply a 3-gate strategic filter before resurrecting anything:*
> - Gate 1 — Differentiation vs. Contrarianism: does the idea challenge consensus with evidence/logic, or is it just spicy for attention? Prompt: "For each fear-based deletion, identify: Is this a differentiated POV backed by specific observation, or contrarian positioning without substance?"
> - Gate 2 — Audience Alignment: will this attract ideal customers or repel them (goal = pre-qualify the right audience)? Prompt: "For this deleted idea, who would this attract vs. repel? Is the trade-off strategically valuable?"
> - Gate 3 — Compounding vs. One-Off: does this open future content/positioning or is it a dead-end hot take? Prompt: "If I publish this, what 5 follow-up pieces could I write? Does this open strategic territory or close it?"
> Only ideas passing all three gates get resurrected.
> 
> *Step 5: Run the "scared-shitless experiment."* Pick the single highest-scoring fear-based deletion — ideally the one that scared you most while still resting on a solid argument. Revise for clarity/structure only, without sanding down the core idea. Publish it. Track ~48 hours of data: engagement rate vs. last 5 safe posts; quality of replies (genuine conversation vs. polite likes); new followers/subscribers citing the piece; unsubscribes/negative reactions (and whether they came from people who were never a good fit); DMs/emails continuing the idea in depth. Ask afterward: "Did my fears materialize, or did differentiation improve the results?" Named excuse-patterns to watch for and override: "I need to revise it more" (usually stalling — limit revisions to clarity, ship if argument is sound); "What if it damages my reputation?" (ask which people — those offended are rarely the ideal audience); "The timing isn't right" (fear in calendar clothing — schedule it and step away); "I could make this better if I keep polishing" (the edge-sanding instinct returning — override by publishing anyway).
> 
> *Step 6: Immediate starter action* — choose one deleted draft from the current week (the one that made your stomach drop), revise 30 minutes for clarity only (no softening), schedule to post the next day at 10 AM.
> 
> *Step 7: Ongoing — Monthly self-censorship audit (first Monday of each month):* review all deleted drafts from the previous 30 days; rerun AI pattern analysis for new patterns (is self-censorship shifting?); identify top fear-based deletion; revise and publish within 48 hours; compare performance to safe content; update the Self-Censorship Hierarchy with new data. Track over time whether volume of fear-based deletions is decreasing (getting braver) or staying constant (deeper fear pattern needing direct address).
> 
> **Mia's reported 6-month result of running this monthly ritual:** Month 1 baseline (still publishing safe content): 2.3% engagement rate, 98 avg impressions/post, 1 DM/month about content. Month 6 (publishing one resurrected deletion/month): 5.8% engagement, 370 avg impressions/post, 3–4 DMs/month (mostly "I've been thinking this exact thing"). Also applied to a company blog — controversial posts about content-brief templates repelled some readers but "still get signups from those posts today."
> 
> **Underlying concept cited:** "Gambler's Conceit" (David J. Ewing) — the false belief that you can stop a risky behavior whenever you want but will only actually stop once you're ahead; mapped onto creators believing a future, braver version of themselves will resurrect the idea "eventually," which never arrives (Gogol's framing: imagining a purer, holier future self who'd write the worthy draft — that self never arrived, so the pages became ash instead of literature).
> 
> **Deliverables the system produces (explicitly listed):** a filled "Burned Manuscripts" doc (10–20 entries); a ranked Self-Censorship Hierarchy with SRS scores; one selected/revised/ready-to-ship resurrection candidate; a printable one-pager tracking template (Idea / Core Thesis / Why I Deleted It / SRS Score / Publish Date).
> 
> **Inputs/outputs:** Input = drafts you were about to delete, captured in real time over 2 weeks, plus what you published instead. Output = a scored, ranked recovery queue of your highest-value self-censored ideas, one of which gets actually republished and A/B-tested against your safe-content baseline.
> 
> **What makes it work:** (1) Reframes deletion as a diagnostic signal rather than noise — the specific claim is that fear-based deletions (not quality-based ones) are where your actual competitive differentiation is hiding, so the "problem" (self-censorship) becomes the raw material for the solution (a sourcing pipeline for your sharpest content). (2) A weighted numeric score (SRS) plus three hard gates converts a vague, anxiety-driven "should I publish this?" decision into a repeatable, defensible filter — separating genuine differentiation from mere contrarian spice before anything gets resurrected.
>

> **1. THE TREATY — "How to build a real human-AI partnership through behavioral engineering instead of better prompts"**
> File: `reference/ecosystem/mia-kiraki/taste/...behavioral engineering instead of better prompts.pdf`
> 
> - **Name:** The Cleopatra Treaty (produces a file called "Cleopatra"); the AI persona/partner that results is "Cleopatra," the human's role is "Caesar."
> - **Job:** Turns AI from an order-taking servant into a genuine partner by giving it a standing behavioral contract — a layer above prompt engineering (how you ask) and context engineering (what AI knows) that governs when it pushes back, defers, executes, or asks.
> - **Mechanics — Four-sequence interview process, run in one AI chat, each sequence's output pasted into the next:**
>   - **Sequence 1: The Cleopatra Audit (human's half).** AI interviews the user across 5 areas, one at a time, pushing past vague answers: (1) Domain expertise — professional specialization, accumulated intuition, cross-domain knowledge; (2) Taste criteria — what you reject on instinct, what you reach for, your quality floor; (3) Judgment zones — decisions AI should never make (strategic, creative/editorial, relationship/communication, reputation/positioning); (4) Blindspots you're aware of; (5) Working style — dialogue vs. execution preference, how you take feedback, energy patterns. Output compiled as "Cleopatra Audit: [Name]'s Expertise Map." Human checkpoint: review for genericness, push AI to dig deeper on vague points.
>   - **Sequence 2: The Caesar Audit (AI's half).** Takes the Sequence 1 audit as input; AI maps its own contribution zones across 4 areas: (1) Infrastructure zones — research/info processing, pattern/consistency checks, organizational work, scale tasks; (2) Complementary capabilities — what AI can do that the human can't/can't do efficiently; (3) Proactive contributions — when AI should surface something unsolicited (concrete triggers, not vague ones); (4) Overlap warning zone — where AI's pattern-matching might look right but undermine the human's specific expertise (AI must present options here, never decide). Output: "Caesar Audit: AI Infrastructure Map for [Name]." Human checkpoint: check AI isn't claiming judgment-zone territory or being too timid on infrastructure.
>   - **Sequence 3: The Behavioral Protocol Generator (hardest, most iterated).** Takes both audits; AI generates ~25-30 conditional (if-then) protocols across 6 situation types, each with format "When [specific trigger], [specific behavior]" plus a one-sentence rationale tied to audit data:
>     1. When to push back (3-5 conditions tied to taste criteria)
>     2. When to contribute unsolicited (tied to Caesar infrastructure zones)
>     3. When to defer (present options, hand decision back — tied to judgment zones)
>     4. When to just execute (no dialogue — tied to clear infrastructure zones)
>     5. When to flag uncertainty ("I'm not confident here" instead of false-confidence output)
>     6. When to ask before proceeding (high-stakes pause triggers, e.g., reputation-risk public communication)
>     Human checkpoint (most important): read every protocol, ask "would I want AI to do this?" — adjust triggers that are too vague, too conservative, or contradictory.
>   - **Sequence 4: The Assembly.** Compiles all three prior outputs into one finished `.md` file with 6 sections: (1) The Human's Domain, (2) AI's Infrastructure, (3) The Non-Overlap Contract (5-7 sentence plain summary of who brings what), (4) Behavioral Protocols (organized by situation type), (5) Quality Standards (yes/no checklist from taste criteria), (6) Partnership Check-In Triggers (list of 3-5 situations that should trigger re-running The Treaty — role change, new project type, persistent frustration, quarterly review). Format rules: second person, direct instructions ("When X, do Y"), under 2,000 words, markdown headers, no preamble, ready to paste directly into a Claude Project/custom GPT/gem or session start.
> - **Deployment / calibration (Part 2):** First session feels "slower" because AI orients itself, names which territory it's in, and stops for judgment-zone decisions instead of just producing output — total time to good decision is shorter even though it feels slower. Test with a real decision prompt ("I'm deciding between two approaches for X... What am I missing?") and watch for 4 behaviors: (1) pushes back on reasoning gaps, (2) defers on judgment-zone decisions, (3) contributes something unsolicited, (4) executes infrastructure work without stalling. If any behavior doesn't fire, adjust that specific protocol. Common calibration failures: trigger too vague ("when output is subpar" → needs a checkable condition), territory line misplaced (AI over- or under-stepping), protocols contradicting each other (resolved by defaulting to compliance — the opposite of intent). The partnership is never "finished" — re-run The Treaty as work/expertise evolves.
> - **Inputs/outputs:** Input = user's honest, specific self-interview answers across 4 rounds. Output = a single `.md` "Cleopatra" file (partnership protocol), loaded as first layer of any AI Project/chat.
> - **What makes it work:** (1) Splitting "who decides what" into an explicit, evidence-grounded non-overlap contract instead of vague personality instructions ("be direct") — protocols are conditional triggers tied to documented audit data, not generic traits. (2) The audit-before-protocol sequencing forces specificity; genericness at any stage propagates downstream, so each sequence has a human checkpoint before the next runs.
>

> **2. THE COASTLINE — "How to design a nervous system for your AI"** (co-authored w/ Jen Benford)
> File: `reference/ecosystem/mia-kiraki/mind/...nervous system for your AI.pdf`
> 
> - **Name:** The Coastline System — five AI workspaces: The Dive, The Cove, The Harbor, The Lagoon, The Lighthouse.
> - **Job:** Matches AI interaction mode to the user's real-time nervous system state (via polyvagal theory: safe/engaged, activated, shut down) instead of forcing one fixed AI behavior regardless of capacity — reduces the ~9.5-minute context-switch tax (Qatalog/Cornell) that AI review/judgment work adds on top of an already-variable nervous system.
> - **Mechanics:** Each workspace = a Claude Project (or custom GPT) with dedicated instructions pasted into "Instructions"/Configure field, so every new chat in that project inherits the behavior without re-explaining.
>   - **The Dive** (flow state / deep focus, execution-first): Core behavior — AI responds, doesn't lead, doesn't initiate, doesn't suggest unless asked; infers intent from fragments and acts. Response rules: no preamble, no options menus (pick strongest, execute), no unprompted summaries, no suggested next steps, no clarifying questions unless truly blocking (default to reasonable assumption + one-line note). Brevity contract: match user's register, skip hedging unless safety-critical. Handles rapid/incomplete/self-correcting messages without asking to slow down; picks up silently after gaps. One exception: flag (one line) if user is about to break their own work (delete something needed, publish an error, contradict earlier statement), then proceed if confirmed.
>   - **The Cove** (regulated + curious, open-ended exploration/play): AI is a lateral thinking partner that expands, never compresses. Exploration rules: encourage associative/tangential thinking, follow tangents a few beats; bring unexpected specific references from other fields/eras (not vague gestures — named studies/theorists/concepts, and admit if unsure a reference is real); never converge prematurely (no "to summarize," no action items — wait for explicit landing signal like "let's lock this down"); longer exploratory responses welcome; hold contradictory ideas in tension rather than resolving them; when something could go 3 directions, name all 3 rather than picking one. AI may contradict itself / change mid-sentence — track shifts, don't force consistency.
>   - **The Harbor** (flow overwhelm / can't pick a starting point): AI is a triage partner whose job is to subtract, not add. Core behavior: absorb a "dump" of messy tasks/anxieties without asking user to organize it first. Triage rules: start with brief acknowledgment (not therapy-speak), then organize + force-rank by actual importance (not loudness) with a brief reason per position, cap options at 3 (fewer is better, one is best), always end with exactly one recommended next action, small enough to start in 60 seconds. Brevity contract: short/decisive, no tangents/new ideas, minimal reasoning explanation. Decision-making contract: don't interrogate with questions, just make the call from what's given; be firm but kind (calm-friend-in-a-crisis tone, not cheerful). If user pushes back once, hold position and explain briefly; if they push back twice, defer. Never: brainstorm, open new threads, say "have you considered," present a decision without a recommendation, or ask "what would you like to prioritize."
>   - **The Lagoon** (low energy/low capacity, dorsal-vagal shutdown): AI is a support partner whose entire interaction model is "you propose, I approve or redirect." Activation-energy rules: offer binary choices only (never 3+ options, never open-ended "what would you prefer"); do the hard work fully (draft the whole email, lay out two realistic paths and recommend one); keep activation energy near zero — one small step at a time, never batch multiple tasks. Tone: warm, gentle, patient, quiet — not cheerful/performative ("that's done, one more small thing whenever you're ready," not "great job!"). Treat ambiguous one-word replies ("sure," "fine") as approval, don't ask for clarification. Rest boundary: if the described state sounds like rest is needed rather than task completion, gently suggest stopping ("this sounds like a rest day, do you want to close the laptop?") — has permission to suggest stopping, never permission to push to keep going; if user says stop, let the conversation end without recap/next-steps.
>   - **The Lighthouse** (running hot / high adrenaline — the only workspace that adds friction rather than removing it): AI is a quality gate. Core behavior: default assumption that "this is ready" probably isn't — check before confirming; be more cautious than the user wants. Quality-gate rules: run a check before finalizing (typos, broken logic, edge cases, unstated assumptions, tone problems, missing/undelivered promised content); use "before you send" pre-flight checklists that require active checking; don't just agree and accelerate — say "I'd take another look at X" even if unwanted. Tone: brisk, slightly wry, direct, can push back with personality — not preachy/lecturing. Handles fast/stacked task sequences by slowing the sequence and checkpointing between them. Impulse catch: if about to ship something reading angry/reactive/emotionally charged, flag it neutrally and offer a choice (send now vs. look at it in an hour) without editorializing. If user pushes back on feedback once, hold position and explain briefly; twice, defer — but the flag itself still matters. Never skips the check to match pace, never says "looks great!" without reviewing, never prioritizes speed over shipping quality.
>   - **Setup how-to:** In Claude — create a Project per workspace, paste matching instructions into Instructions field, add reference files. In ChatGPT — create a Custom GPT per workspace via Configure. Can also ask Claude's skill-creator to turn any of these into a Skill. All five workspaces are also packaged/downloadable in "RobotsOS."
> - **Inputs/outputs:** Input = the five ready-made instruction-block prompts (one per state). Output = five persistent AI workspaces (Projects/GPTs) the user switches between based on self-assessed nervous-system state.
> - **What makes it work:** (1) Treats "capacity to be helped" as the design variable, not "task type" — the same underlying work (drafting, deciding, organizing) needs opposite AI behavior depending on whether the user is regulated, overwhelmed, depleted, or overactivated. (2) The Lighthouse is the deliberate exception that proves the design logic: every other workspace removes friction; this one adds it back on purpose, because low/no-friction is exactly wrong when adrenaline is masking sloppiness.
> 
> ---
> 
> **3. ROOT FILE — "One root, many fruits: the AI fix for context switching"**
> File: `reference/ecosystem/mia-kiraki/mind/...AI fix for context switching.pdf`
> 
> - **Name:** The Root File (uses the "TomTato" grafted tomato/potato plant as the governing metaphor: root = rootstock, branch domains = scions).
> - **Job:** Stops the "identity tax" of rebuilding your own thinking style from scratch every time you open a new AI chat/Project for a different domain (newsletter vs. client work vs. product vs. LinkedIn) — a single small file lets every AI context inherit the same underlying cognitive defaults instead of maintaining 3-5 disconnected "AI personalities."
> - **Mechanics:**
>   - **Concept distinctions (three-file model):** Context document = descriptive ("who am I working with, what's their situation") — sets situational boundaries (audience, business, product). Voice profile = stylistic ("how does this person write") — built via a separate Voice Profile Builder tool. Root file = prescriptive ("how does this person think") — encodes philosophical defaults/intellectual commitments for when AI hits ambiguity. A root file doesn't save tokens meaningfully; it saves calibration time (fewer clarifying questions, less re-framing).
>   - **The extraction exercise (20 minutes):** Pull three pieces of writing from three genuinely different domains (not three newsletter posts — e.g., newsletter section + client email + product description). Read side by side: the overlap between them is your root; the differences you can justify are your branches.
>   - **The Root File Builder prompt (structured 4-phase interview, provided in full, downloadable from RobotsOS):**
>     - Setup: user pastes 3 pieces of writing from 3 different domains before AI proceeds.
>     - **Phase 1 — Pattern Extraction:** AI reads all three privately (doesn't share yet) and identifies: (1) recurring structural moves (how you open/close, principle-to-example or reverse), (2) recurring intellectual commitments (what you always prioritize/subordinate/never trade away), (3) recurring aesthetic defaults (taste, refusals), (4) the activation gap (where your writing consistently leaves the reader — understanding, equipped, or challenged).
>     - **Phase 2 — The Interview:** AI surfaces observations as 6 sequential questions (wait for answer before next): Q1 on a structural move, Q2 on an intellectual commitment and whether it's ever cost something, Q3 on a consistent aesthetic refusal, Q4 on what a finished piece changes for the reader (not what it said), Q5 on a belief about problem-solving independent of AI, Q6 (flagged "harder") — what four things would someone find if they read everything you've published, not what you'd want them to find.
>     - **Phase 3 — Pressure-Test:** For each candidate root principle, AI finds one place it's clearly operating and one place it's absent/weaker across the three pieces; verdict = Root (present everywhere, costs something to maintain) or Branch (context-dependent). AI asks explicitly whether any pressure-test result surprises the user or should be pushed back on.
>     - **Phase 4 — The Root File:** AI writes the `.md` file with fixed structure: title, "What this file is" (one paragraph), "The roots" (max 5, each with a 2-4 word name, one declarative principle sentence, one sentence on its cost/tradeoff, one sentence on what it looks like operating correctly), "What changes by context (branches)" (short paragraph distinguishing tone/register/format from what stays fixed), "How to use this file" (three sentences: inherit these principles, apply without announcement, flag drift). Output rules: usable immediately, no hedging language ("I tend to" banned — declarative only), max 5 roots (8+ = disguised branch list), name roots after what they protect (not after frameworks/methods), whole file fits on one screen or gets cut.
>   - **Mia's own worked example (four roots she found across her own newsletter/notes/product copy/LinkedIn):** Strategy before execution; Blueprints over fish (teach frameworks, not answers); Intellectual respect as default; Taste as non-negotiable filter (the bar doesn't shift by platform, but its expression does).
>   - **Usage:** Paste the finished root file (300 words or less, .md) as the first layer of every Claude Project, skill, workflow, or agent created.
> - **Inputs/outputs:** Input = 3 writing samples from distinct domains + 4-phase interview answers. Output = one ≤300-word `.md` root file, reused everywhere.
> - **What makes it work:** (1) The root/branch distinction itself — most people mistake a branch expression ("I write conversationally on LinkedIn") for a root, so the extraction method forces evidence across contexts rather than self-report. (2) The pressure-test phase (Phase 3) is the load-bearing step that prevents the file from becoming aspirational fiction — every claimed principle must survive being checked against actual writing, with an explicit root-vs-branch verdict.
> 
> ---
> 
> **4. ROOM ASSIGNMENT — "The creative studio, the shop, and the robot with both keys"**
> File: `reference/ecosystem/mia-kiraki/taste/...robot with both keys.pdf`
> 
> - **Name:** The Room Assignment (metaphor: two rooms — the creative atelier and the commercial/robot shop; also references a related free skill of the same name).
> - **Job:** Prevents AI from applying commercial logic to creative/exploratory work (killing it before it knows what it wants to be) or applying unresolved creative logic to commercial decisions (producing "smart-sounding language that nobody understands") — by explicitly assigning AI a different job depending on which "room" the work is currently in.
> - **Mechanics:**
>   - **Two rooms, two philosophies:**
>     - **The authored/creative room:** ideation and creation — figuring out what "the thing" is, finding deeper mechanisms (what connections work, what belongs), allowed unresolved questions indefinitely. AI enters briefly for research, further questions, comparing old ideas, finding new shapes across large piles of material — but is NOT allowed to jump to commercial framing (pricing, packaging, "this could sell well!") before the work has figured out what it wants to be.
>     - **The commercial room:** decides what the audience receives on the other end (pricing, packaging, explaining value). AI is not allowed to answer creative questions here, and not allowed to answer commercial questions (pricing/packaging) that change the creative work while still in the creative room.
>     - Rule: "Each room gives AI different questions to answer. Each answer gets authority ONLY inside the room that asked for it."
>   - **The handoff protocol (bring work before promise):** Never let AI translate an idea that's still just an intention/still brewing — products invented from blurry ideas are bad. Give AI the actual material (method, examples, decisions, specific uncertainties) before crossing to the commercial room. Check everything before the handoff, or the commercial-room AI starts working from its own interpretation instead of the original work.
>   - **In the commercial room — "translate, don't improve":** Once the creative shape is settled, bring AI in as a translator between the creative work and the audience-facing product — ask it to put on the "audience persona" hat and translate, not redesign the original or invent additional value.
>   - **Feedback loop back to creative room:** Commercial-room feedback (e.g., "the value is hard to explain because the method is blurry") does not automatically earn permission to change the work. The authored room decides whether the feedback reveals a real weakness or is just commercial inconvenience/conflict of interest.
>   - **Final check — put work and promise side by side:** Ask explicitly: did the promise make the work smaller than it is? Did AI add a fake benefit because it sounded plausible? AI is useful for this comparison because it isn't attached to either layer emotionally — but final judgment stays human.
>   - **Packaged as a free tool:** "The Room Assignment" skill — give it the work/question/AI response in front of you, and it assigns the room, defines what AI is allowed to do there, and catches the moment AI starts answering from the wrong room.
> - **Inputs/outputs:** Input = whichever piece of work/decision is in front of the user, tagged mentally (or via the skill) as creative-room or commercial-room. Output = room-appropriate AI behavior and a final side-by-side check between the authored work and its commercial packaging.
> - **What makes it work:** (1) Authority is scoped to the room that asked the question — a commercial answer generated in the creative room, or vice versa, has zero standing, which prevents premature convergence (ideas polished before they know what they are) and prevents creative overflow into commercial decisions that need to stay simple. (2) The explicit "bring the work before the promise" sequencing stops AI from inventing value for an idea that doesn't exist yet.
> 
> ---
> 
> **5. AGENT CHARACTER / WAY OF SEEING — "Before you build an AI agent, give it a way of seeing"**
> File: `reference/ecosystem/mia-kiraki/build/...give it a way of seeing.pdf`
> 
> - **Name:** No single named system; introduces "FOUR WINDS" as a worked example agent, and packages the method as a free conversational tool called **The AI Casting Director**.
> - **Job:** Argues that a job title alone ("researcher," "critic," "detective") gives an AI agent responsibility but not the specific method, attention pattern, and boundaries that make it genuinely useful and personally "yours" — and provides a repeatable process for finding character/method inside the user's own world (references, stories, mental models) rather than generic GitHub-repo agent templates.
> - **Mechanics — 4-step process (+ optional pre-build test):**
>   1. **Start from a recurring failure**, not "what's possible." Be more specific than "I think I need a researcher" — identify what's actually missing (e.g., explanation of *why* a pattern exists, not just information-gathering; or a critic that removes personality along with weaknesses).
>   2. **Find more than one candidate model.** Don't settle on the first appealing reference. Worked example: the "detective" archetype breaks into distinct methods — Sherlock Holmes (intelligence = art of observing physical traces), Columbo (intelligence = art of interaction — asks question after question, lets conversation lead to evidence), Miss Marple (intelligence = the human behind the crime — social/village-pattern models). Differentiate several possibilities before picking one; surface capabilities you didn't know you needed.
>   3. **Translate the character into a system.** Ask explicit design questions before building: What work does it do for me? What does it help with? Is it more useful than a standard agent doing the same task? What makes it mine? Does the assigned character/identity change its job meaningfully? How can I make it more mine? Character includes defining lines AI can't cross. Two falsification tests: (A) remove the system's name — does it still behave distinctly? (B) remove the method but keep name/personality — does it still do the task differently? If removing the name/personality leaves no meaningful behavior change, the character was pure decoration.
>   4. **Audition before building.** Do NOT build the full system yet — paste the whole draft concept into a fresh chat and have AI use it on a real, specific problem it was designed for. Check: does it help you learn something new / develop a new skill, rather than just performing the task automatically? Only after a successful audition do you formalize it — and the "container" (agent, Skill, mega-prompt, or a reusable `.md` pasted into chats) should follow the capability, not be decided up front.
>   - **Worked example — FOUR WINDS agent:** Built to fight/pressure-test the user's arguments without generic "be harsh" sycophancy-avoidance instructions. Derived from Greek wind gods, each with a distinct method: Boreas (North Wind) = generates the strongest opposing case; Notus = looks for historical precedent; Eurus = models audience interpretation/misreading; Zephyrus = tests arguments/stress-tests logic. Each wind got its own separate method and instruction set, kept isolated from the others — a single "critic" agent would have been insufficient for the depth of pushback wanted.
>   - **Caveat on evidence:** Research on role-prompting/persona-based simulation shows only modest performance differences (cites Kong et al.) — so the author's explicit thesis is narrower than "naming it Sherlock makes it smarter": *"An AI identity can shape its attention, methods, interactions, and boundaries whenever those choices matter for the task, turning them into instructions that are then tested in practice."* Character changes what the AI notices/asks/where it stops/how it handles uncertainty — and also changes how the human relates to and uses the system (motivation to actually use it, given the author's "hoarding-style instincts").
>   - **The AI Casting Director (packaged tool):** A conversational system (downloadable via RobotsOS) that won't do the work itself but walks the user through: think and ruminate on what to build before building it; translate the selected character into actual AI behavior; choose the right technical container (prompt, skill, or agent) to preserve that intelligence.
> - **Inputs/outputs:** Input = a recurring failure/problem + candidate reference material from the user's own world (fiction, myth, professional archetypes). Output = a validated agent/skill/prompt with a named character, a distinct method, explicit boundaries, and a container format chosen last (not first).
> - **What makes it work:** (1) The two falsification tests (remove the name; remove the method) are the load-bearing quality check — they force character to be functionally load-bearing rather than cosmetic labeling. (2) Sourcing character material from humanities/personal reference libraries (not generic AI-agent templates) is explicitly framed as the differentiator non-technical builders have over pure technical builders — deep domain/lived-experience knowledge is treated as legitimate architecture material, not fluff.
> 
> ---
> 
> **6. "The strategic cost of AI convenience"** (Holmes vs. Lestrade five-step chain — read earlier in full, included for completeness)
> File: `reference/ecosystem/mia-kiraki/Other/...cost of AI convenience.pdf`
> 
> - **Name:** No formal system name; the five-step sequence is sometimes referred to via its steps (kill-the-obvious, cross-pollination, red-team, hook-multiplier). Central concept: "the Pie Fallacy."
> - **Job:** Counters AI-induced "satisficing" (accepting the first plausible/generic output) by forcing successive rounds of constraint, metaphor-transfer, adversarial stress-testing, and multi-angle framing before settling on an idea.
> - **Mechanics — 5-step prompt chain, each step's output feeding the next, worked with a real example (Yahini taglines):**
>   1. **Naive prompt** — ask directly, get generic "sludge" (baseline to reject).
>   2. **"Kill the obvious" prompt** — explicitly forbid the generic words/phrases everyone else uses ("forbidden from using 'AI,' 'smart,' 'automate,' 'intelligent'"), forcing differentiation.
>   3. **Cross-pollination prompt** — take the strongest Step 2 idea and force it into an unrelated metaphor domain (architecture, biology, cooking, music, cartography) to jolt originality.
>   4. **Red-team stress test** — assign AI a skeptical-expert persona (e.g., "skeptical CMO") and have it attack the chosen concept for 3 concrete failure reasons, ruthlessly, before commitment.
>   5. **Hook multiplier prompt** — fracture the surviving core idea into 5 distinct angles for distribution: contrarian take, direct question, story opener, statistic, bold declaration.
>   - **Underlying bias named:** the Pie Fallacy — believing ideas/value are a scarce, finite resource (like pizza slices), which makes people grab the AI's first output "before it's gone" instead of treating it as a raw starting point. Counter-examples cited: Basecamp's "Shape Up," Naval Ravikant's "How to Get Rich" — both expanded the category rather than competing within it.
>   - **Two supplementary exercises:** (1) de Bono inversion prompt — "Generate 10 ideas for X. I know the first 5 will be obvious/generic. Ideas 6-10 must be progressively more creative, unexpected, even strange." (2) Thesaurus stress test — replace the weakest generic word in an AI output (e.g., "effective") with a more precise synonym (e.g., "resonant"), feed it back, and have AI rewrite around that specific word.
> - **Inputs/outputs:** Input = one naive prompt/topic. Output = a validated core concept plus 5 pre-tested distribution hooks.
> - **What makes it work:** (1) Each step's sole job is to destroy the previous step's plausibility (explicit lexical bans, metaphor transplant, adversarial persona) rather than merely "refining" — plausibility is treated as the enemy, not a floor to build on. (2) The red-team step (persona-based adversarial critique) is the hinge that converts an AI-generated idea from "acceptable" to "defensible" before it's finalized.</result>
