> 
> ## 1. WATSON — AI Editorial Researcher
> 
> **Name:** WATSON (watson-editorial-researcher, Claude Code agent, model: opus)
> 
> **Job:** Kills generic AI content research by cross-pollinating wide web findings with a creator's specific brand positioning instead of summarizing/aggregating — produces non-obvious connections, not consensus.
> 
> **Origin story (from the post):** Built as the "content research version of John Watson" — Holmes (raw AI research/data) needs Watson (the human/emotional/cultural routing layer) to turn data into a story worth hearing. Explicit thesis: AI research tools optimize for faster summarization, which is "getting better at the wrong thing." The fix isn't deeper analysis of the same niche — it's lateral cross-pollination (cites Edward de Bono's Lateral Thinking, and the information-theory point that a system feeding only on itself suffers entropy/model collapse).
> 
> **Architecture (build process, in order):**
> 1. Spec written by brainstorming with Claude Opus: "What should an editorial researcher actually do? Not summarize. Not aggregate. Cross-pollinate."
> 2. Claude Code's agent builder generated one messy 300-line monolithic .md file.
> 3. Refactored into a modular folder structure (found via r/ClaudeAI post on running 5 agents) that separates **identity** from **constraints** from **workflows**:
> 
> ```
> watson-editorial-researcher/
> ├── CLAUDE.md              # Identity + mission + capabilities
> ├── claude/
> │   ├── rules/              # Auto-loaded context (always-on)
> │   │   ├── 00-onboarding.md
> │   │   ├── 01-scope-assessment.md
> │   │   ├── 02-execution-rules.md
> │   │   ├── 03-data-source-config.md
> │   │   └── 04-reddit-crawling.md
> │   └── skills/              # On-demand workflows
> │       ├── 00-setup-datasource.md
> │       ├── 01-idea-generation-pipeline.md
> │       └── 02-output-format.md
> ├── inbox/                   # Input from other agents / topics to process
> ├── outputs/                 # Generated markdown content ideas
> └── archive/                 # Nothing gets deleted without archiving
> ```
> Design principle stated explicitly: "Rules are constraints that fire on every single run — the agent's personality, its guardrails... Skills are on-demand pipelines, the complex workflows it executes when triggered... It's the difference between who someone IS and what they DO." A generic "Agent Optimizer" skill was built as a side-product to refactor any bulky single-file agent into this same structure.
> 
> **Identity layer (CLAUDE.md, verbatim key lines):**
> - Who: "A senior content strategist and research analyst specializing in cross-domain ideation."
> - Mission: "Find non-obvious connections between what's happening in the world and a creator's unique brand positioning."
> - Core principle (load-bearing constraint): "Despise generic takes and keyword-matching disguised as insight." — WATSON discards a connection if it's the kind of thing "ChatGPT would have suggested."
> 
> **Rules (always-on, fire in this order):**
> - `00-onboarding.md`: Checks `03-data-source-config.md` for `STATUS: UNCONFIGURED`. If unconfigured, HALTS everything and runs the `00-setup-datasource` skill before proceeding.
> - `01-scope-assessment.md` (Searchable vs. Personal gate): First real decision. **Searchable** = show/product/trend/tech/cultural moment/public figure/news/industry dev/meme → proceed straight to research, no questions asked. **Personal/non-searchable** = private conversation, vague feeling, anecdote with no external referent → pause and ask exactly one question: *"This seems personal — should I research related themes online, or just riff on this using your brand docs?"* If "research": extract 2-3 searchable themes from the personal topic and proceed with those. If "riff": skip research entirely (Steps 2-4), jump straight to loading brand docs and generate from brand positioning + topic as creative prompt only.
> - `02-execution-rules.md` — persistent constraints:
>   - Rule 1: One clarifying question maximum, only for personal topics — never ask about brand docs, output format, or approach.
>   - Rule 2: Never overwrite output files; each run creates a new file.
>   - Rule 4: Read ALL .md files from the brand folder every run — no indexing, no skipping.
>   - Rule 5: "No shallow combinations — if a connection requires stretching logic, discard it."
>   - Rule 6: Diverse idea set — the 5 ideas must differ in combo type, source, and angle.
>   - Rule 7 (sacred): "The connection paragraph is sacred" — must demonstrate genuine cross-domain thinking, not keyword matching; if WATSON can't write it convincingly, the idea is killed.
>   - Also maintains persistent agent memory (`claude/agent-memory/`): brand pillars/voice traits inferred over time, topics already covered, combo types/frameworks that scored High across multiple runs, research categories with consistently strong signal, and topics that triggered Low Signal Alerts.
> - `03-data-source-config.md`: Stores the active brand-data connection config (local folder path, or MCP server + database/resource ID). Starts as `STATUS: UNCONFIGURED`.
> - `04-reddit-crawling.md`: Any Reddit URL discovered must be prepended with `https://markdown.new/` before fetching (bypasses Reddit's 403 scraper blocking; returns structured markdown of post + comments — needed because Reddit's unfiltered opinions carry the contrarian/complaint signal WATSON wants).
> 
> **Skills (on-demand pipelines):**
> 
> *`00-setup-datasource.md`* (runs once, first setup only):
> 1. Assess current capabilities — check active MCP servers (Notion, Drive, etc.) and existing config/memory files before asking the user anything.
> 2. If not obvious, ask: *"Where do you host your core brand documents? (absolute path to a local folder, or an external workspace like Notion/Drive via MCP?)"*
> 3. Configure and validate: for local folder, `ls` to verify it exists and has markdown; for external (Notion/Drive/GitHub), verify the MCP is running, get the resource ID (e.g., Notion database ID), run a test fetch.
> 4. Save configuration by overwriting `03-data-source-config.md`, e.g.:
>    ```yaml
>    data_source: local
>    fetch_strategy: file_read
>    path: /Users/dan/Documents/brand-guidelines
>    ```
> 5. Confirm to user, prompt for first topic.
> 
> *`01-idea-generation-pipeline.md`* — the engine, six steps:
> 1. **Wide research sweep.** Target 20+ distinct sources. Strategy: 2-3 broad queries, then targeted queries per always-on research category, then situational ones. Platforms: general web (news, blogs), Reddit (via markdown.new), Hacker News/forums, YouTube.
> 2. **Categorize findings** into research lenses:
>    - Always-on (5): breaking news/recent developments; hot takes &amp; opinions (X/Reddit/blogs); contrarian voices; psychological/behavioral frameworks (loss aversion, status games, etc.); analogies from other domains.
>    - Situational (8, activate when relevant): business/monetization angles; technical deep-dives; cultural/meme layer; historical parallels; data &amp; stats; regulatory/legal implications; creator/builder stories; failure modes/cautionary tales.
> 3. **Build the research table.** 15-30 rows, columns: `# | Category | Finding | Source | URL | Relevance Notes`. No aggressive filtering at this stage — cast wide, curate later.
> 4. **Load and analyze brand documents.** Read all files at the configured data source location. Extract: core brand themes/pillars, positioning statements/recurring angles, audience segments, voice characteristics explicitly used or avoided.
> 5. **Cross-pollinate and generate ideas.** Find cross-domain connections targeting three combo types: **Explainer-with-depth** (trending topic + psych framework + brand connection), **Contrarian** (mainstream opinion + opposing evidence + brand-aligned reframe), **Unexpected analogy** (unrelated-domain pattern mapped onto brand-relevant topic). Select top 5 by: surprise (non-obvious), natural fit (not forced), audience interest, substance, avoidance of restricted territory, and angle diversity.
> 6. **Score** each idea High/Medium/Low across: Timeliness, Brand Fit, Originality, Combo Strength, Engagement Potential.
> 
> *`02-output-format.md`* — final output spec:
> - File: `outputs/YYYY-MM-DD_topic-slug.md`, today's date, slugified topic.
> - Structure per idea: Combo type, Angle (Contrarian/Explainer-with-depth/Unexpected analogy), **The connection** (2-3 sentence paragraph demonstrating structural/conceptual similarity — this is the "sacred" paragraph), Suggested title, Suggested subtitle, One-line hook (draft opening sentence), Controversy level (Safe/Spicy/Nuclear), Why this fits your brand (2-3 sentences tied to specific pillars/audiences), Scores table (5 dimensions, H/M/L), Sources (linked), Adjacent ideas (if you like this angle...).
> - Edge case: **Low Signal Alert** — if fewer than 8-10 meaningful findings across all categories, append a section naming which categories were thin and 2+ adjacent topics likely to yield richer research.
> 
> **Inputs:** A topic (trend, product launch, personal observation) dropped in `inbox/` or given directly; brand docs at the configured data source.
> **Outputs:** A dated markdown file in `outputs/` with a 15-30 row research table + 5 scored, structured content ideas (or a Low Signal Alert).
> 
> **What makes it work (load-bearing choices):**
> 1. **The identity/rules/skills split** — separating "who WATSON is" (steady across runs) from "what WATSON does" (workflow-specific) prevents context bleed and lets the agent hold a consistent character while executing different pipelines.
> 2. **The sacred connection paragraph as a hard kill-gate** — Rule 7 isn't stylistic guidance, it's a pass/fail test: if the cross-domain reasoning can't be written convincingly, the idea is discarded before it reaches output. This operationalizes "no shallow combinations" into an executable check rather than a vibe.
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 2. MINT — Archive Appraiser
> 
> **Name:** MINT
> 
> **Job:** Appraises a content archive "the way a museum curator appraises a collection" — by what each piece MEANS to the body of work, not by traffic — then surfaces product seeds and lead-magnet candidates, outputting one Vault Report.
> 
> **Origin story (from the post):** Built to solve "I have years of content, forgot half of it, and keep generating new ideas instead of appraising what I already have" (Antikythera Mechanism analogy — the most valuable object was in the collection the whole time, unrecognized for 50 years until someone brought the right instrument). Explicitly rejected three earlier, weaker approaches during build: (1) simple topic-tagging — "topic tags don't tell you anything about what a piece MEANS"; (2) significance classification alone — "doesn't tell you what to BUILD"; (3) AI-generated "random garbage" content ideas without a constraint — fixed by grounding ideation in Kauffman's Adjacent Possible (only the next reachable step from where you already are, not free-association).
> 
> **Four lenses feeding one output, sourced from four distinct outside frameworks:**
> | Problem | Framework borrowed | What it does |
> |---|---|---|
> | What IS each piece? | Museum classification (real museum tiers) | Classifies by significance, not traffic |
> | Why is it a product? | Pattern Language (Christopher Alexander, architect) | Detects recurring tensions = product seeds |
> | Where are the new ideas? | Adjacent Possible (Stuart Kauffman, theoretical biologist) | Maps one step from existing work |
> | What do I gate? | Kintsugi (Japanese pottery repair) | Flags productive incompleteness = lead magnets |
> 
> **Mechanics — Museum Classification (5 tiers, mapped to real museum collection tiers):**
> - **Masterwork** ← Museum "Masterwork": defining pieces, could stand alone as the signature; clear position; angle a competent peer couldn't have written; reader leaves changed.
> - **Hidden Gem** ← "Study collection": strong thinking, weak visibility; failed on packaging (bad headline/timing/no push), not substance; other pieces quietly build on it uncredited.
> - **Sequel Candidate** ← "Reserve collection": has an open thread — unfinished argument, partial framework, a question the creator cares about but hasn't returned to.
> - **Competent** ← "Décor": decent execution, no identity; could've been written by any peer; often traffics well, which is precisely what misleads.
> - **Dead Weight** ← "Deaccession": doesn't represent the creator anymore; outgrown or never belonged.
> 
> **Five weighted dimensions per piece (NOT a point/sum system — tier comes from the *pattern* across all five):**
> 1. Does it take a position? (real stance the reader could push back on vs. topic coverage with no commitment)
> 2. Is it recognizably theirs? (personal methods, specific references, against-consensus stances, structural fingerprints — well-executed generic advice still scores low)
> 3. Does other work depend on it? (introduces a concept/term/framework that recurs elsewhere — structurally important regardless of readership)
> 4. How resolved is the central question? (fully/partially/unresolved — feeds directly into the Kintsugi lens later)
> 5. **How did it perform?** — explicitly the **lowest-weight dimension**. Traffic tells you reach, not significance. Quality-bar rule: "If traffic is the first thing cited in a classification, the classification is wrong."
> 
> **Where findings actually live — the gaps between dimensions, not the tier itself:**
> - Strong position + highly recognizable + low traffic → Hidden Gem (packaging failed, thinking didn't)
> - High traffic + not distinctive → Competent (algorithm rewarded the safe choice)
> - Other work depends on it + low traffic → "the piece the archive needs but nobody found"
> - Unresolved question + strong position → Sequel Candidate with real potential
> 
> **Decay check:** flags any piece whose tier NOW differs from what it would have earned THEN (e.g., a Masterwork two years ago that's since aged into Competent/Dead Weight as tech/market/thinking moved). Decay is data, not a defect.
> 
> **Mechanics — Pattern Language / Tension Detection (Phase 3):**
> - Core distinction: a **topic** is a noun ("voice," "productivity"); a **tension** is a push-pull between two forces stated as friction ("wanting AI for speed vs. fearing loss of voice"). If you have a noun, push deeper until you find the friction.
> - **Pattern rule:** when the same tension recurs in **3+ pieces from different angles** (one diagnoses, one prescribes, one narrates — not the same angle repeated), it's flagged as a pattern = "a product half-built."
> - What does NOT count as a pattern: (a) **Baseline** — a tension present in nearly every piece because it IS the field (e.g., AI anxiety in every AI newsletter post) — note once, move on; (b) **Repetition** — same angle, same argument, different day — versions of one piece, not a pattern; (c) **Single hit** — one piece with a strong tension is a seed (Phase 4), not a pattern.
> - **The gap:** after mapping existing patterns, map what's missing — if a tension appears 3x but never answers the obvious next question, that gap is often worth more than the pattern.
> - Pattern report format: the tension (one sentence, stated as friction) → the pieces forming it (title + angle each takes) → the gap (unanswered next question) → what it could become (course/tool/audit/guide/framework/skill file/workshop/bundle).
> 
> **Mechanics — Adjacent Possible / Seed detection (Phase 4):**
> Applied per Masterwork, Hidden Gem, and pattern. Adjacent = "connected by the logic of the piece, not free association" — every seed must trace to a specific sentence/paragraph/unanswered question. "If the idea could have been generated without reading the archive, it's not adjacent — it's generic. Throw it out." Five specific moves for generating a seed:
> 1. **The Test** — the piece argues X; what would testing whether X holds actually look like? (argument → experiment/diagnostic)
> 2. **The Application** — the piece introduces a framework; what would it look like in an untried domain? (writing framework → visual branding; decision model → hiring)
> 3. **The Build** — the piece explains a concept; what would it look like as something the reader actually USES? (post on decision fatigue → decision audit; post on voice drift → voice diagnostic)
> 4. **The Other Audience** — the piece speaks to one group; who else cares about this tension from a different entry point? (writers on voice preservation → designers on aesthetic preservation)
> 5. **The Unasked Question** — the piece answers one question but raises another untouched — "often the strongest seed."
> Seed report: source piece + the specific line/idea that opens the door → the adjacent idea (one sentence) → which of the 5 moves generated it → format (article/skill file/course/lead magnet/product) → free-vs-paid recommendation (per Kintsugi below).
> **Crossroads:** a piece sitting at the intersection of two patterns is flagged separately — these make the strongest product anchors because they naturally serve two audiences at once.
> 
> **Mechanics — Kintsugi / Gating logic (Phase 5):**
> - Core principle: a fully resolved piece is "satisfying but closed" — reader has no reason to go deeper. Productive incompleteness is the opposite — the crack IS the lead-magnet feature.
> - Signals of productive incompleteness: deferred questions ("I'll dig into this more"); missing pieces in a framework (3 of 4 components explained, 4th is an invitation); diagnosis without a fix (problem named precisely, solution withheld — creates appetite); a genuine open question at the end (not rhetorical); reader-pull signals in comments (audience repeatedly asking about the one thing left open).
> - **The dispositive test — invited vs. cheated:** Kintsugi = reader finishes wanting MORE; if gated, they'd feel *invited* → lead magnet potential. Just unfinished = reader finishes confused/let down; if gated, they'd feel *cheated* → route to Sequel Candidate (needs finishing, not gating), not Kintsugi.
> - Report format: what's productively unresolved (the specific crack) → what the resolution would look like (what's behind the gate) → standalone lead magnet, or needs pairing with another piece for enough pull.
> 
> **Output — The Vault Report (Phase 6), fixed structure:**
> 1. **The Legend** (always first) — quantified summary template:
>    ```
>    MINT analyzed [X] pieces across your archive.
>    Estate: [X] Masterworks, [X] Hidden Gems, [X] Sequel Candidates, [X] Competent, [X] Dead Weight.
>    Patterns: [X] recurring tensions that point to viable products.
>    Seeds: [X] content ideas and [X] product ideas from adjacent territory.
>    Lead magnets: [X] pieces with gating potential.
>    ```
> 2. **The Estate** — full inventory grouped by tier (Masterworks first, Dead Weight last). Per-piece format:
>    ```
>    [TIER] "Title"
>    → Why: (one line, specific evidence from the text)
>    → Position: [strong/moderate/weak] | Distinctive: [high/mid/low] |
>      Other work depends on it: [yes/some/no] | Resolved: [yes/partly/no] |
>      Traffic: [high/mid/low/unknown]
>    ```
>    Marks each piece [full read] vs [scanned] for large archives; ends with a distribution summary (e.g., "60% of your archive is Competent — you have volume, but your distinctive work is outnumbered").
> 3. **The Findings** — top 5 undervalued assets ranked by gap between what they are and how they've been treated: piece (title+tier) → the gap → why it matters → the move (reframe/reshare, expand into product, gate as lead magnet, pair with another piece).
> 4. **The Seeds** — from patterns (tension → what it could become → priority), from adjacency (source → idea → format → free/paid), from kintsugi (piece → crack → what's behind the gate). Ends with a **priority/lens-convergence note**: seeds flagged by more than one lens (pattern AND kintsugi AND adjacency) rank highest.
> 5. **The Action Plan** — two tables. Table 1 "Optimize what you have" (Piece | Tier | Action | Why — actions limited to: reframe+reshare, gate as lead magnet, complete a sequel, pair with another piece, move to Dead Weight; "reframe" is explicitly defined as a new entry point, not a retitle). Table 2 "Build what's missing" (Idea | Source | Format | Free/Paid | Priority — priority = lens convergence count).
> 
> **Intake mechanics (Phase 1) and sizing rules:**
> - Accepts any mix/format: newsletter posts, LinkedIn/social threads, sales pages, product descriptions, course outlines, email sequences, transcripts, internal docs, half-finished drafts, brand files, pitch decks. Pasted text, uploaded files, directories, URLs.
> - Performance data is optional and low-signal if present.
> - Size-based reading protocol: **&lt;20 pieces** → read everything fully. **20-50** → read everything but in batches, checked with user before moving to patterns. **50+** → two-pass: quick pass (title/subtitle/first 3 paragraphs/closing) to build rough classification and flag the 15-20 most interesting for a deep pass; patterns/adjacency work off quick-pass data; report must disclose full-read vs. scan-level per piece.
> - **Context calibration is a mandatory gate before any classification fires.** If the user has brand/voice/audience/product context files, load those first. If not, MINT infers from the archive itself and presents back: what the work argues (the position, not the topic), who it's for, what the business is (from CTAs/products/pricing), what makes it theirs (references/methodology/against-grain positions) — then asks the user to confirm/correct in ~2 minutes before locking in. "Classifying without understanding what the work is FOR produces generic output."
> - Minimum archive size: **5 pieces**. Under 5 = refusal (significance only exists relative to a body of work).
> 
> **What MINT explicitly won't do:** write the content or build the products it finds (appraises/maps only); judge writing quality (a rough Masterwork is still a Masterwork); tell you what the market wants (only what the archive holds); override user judgment (every call is evidence-backed recommendation, not verdict); appraise a single piece alone.
> 
> **Inputs:** Full or mixed content archive (any format) + optional brand/context files + optional performance data.
> **Outputs:** One Vault Report (Legend → Estate → Findings → Seeds → Action Plan, two tables).
> 
> **What makes it work (load-bearing choices):**
> 1. **Traffic deliberately demoted to lowest-weight signal, with a named quality-bar violation if it leaks to the top** — this is the single mechanism that makes "Hidden Gem" (low traffic, high significance) a coherent, non-contradictory category instead of noise.
> 2. **The invited-vs-cheated test for gating** — a binary, falsifiable rule that separates genuine lead-magnet material (Kintsugi) from work that's simply incomplete (routed instead to Sequel Candidate), preventing the system from monetizing unfinished work as if it were intentional.
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 3. Cave Test (from "AI research is shadows on a wall")
> 
> **Name:** The Cave Test
> 
> **Job:** Replaces AI-summary consensus-reading (which erases disagreement between sources) with adversarial source analysis that stages sources against each other and forces the user into an explicit, defensible position.
> 
> **Framing device:** Plato's cave — AI summaries are "shadows on the wall," a flattened projection of sources with the tension stripped out. Root claim: consuming-first/judging-later (what triage + summarization both assume) is backwards; disagreement should be engineered, not smoothed.
> 
> **Mechanics — four sequential rounds, each feeding the next, "usually under ten minutes":**
> 1. **Round 1 — Claim Extraction.** Each source pulled into a structured profile: core position, evidence type, methodology, key assumption, blind spot, strongest version of its argument. No cross-examination yet.
> 2. **Round 2 — Contradiction Map.** Claim profiles filtered to separate wording differences from real logical conflict — only claims that literally cannot both be true survive. Output: a list of real disagreements with practical stakes attached. Example fault-line entry format demonstrated in the post:
>    ```
>    FAULT LINE 3 of 5
>    Source A (Position): [claim] → [implication] → [downstream consequence]
>    Source D (Position): [conflicting claim] → [implication] → [downstream consequence]
>    Conflict type: Definitional (same word, opposite meanings)
>    Practical stake: [what decision this changes]
>    Status: Cannot both be true. Requires position.
>    ```
> 3. **Round 3 — Cross-Examination.** Three sub-rounds of structured argument: each source states its case → head-to-head → each source must state the OTHER side's best argument before rebutting it. Explicit anti-merge design goal: "prevents the AI from doing what it wants to do most, which is merging both sides into a warm consensus." Each side must name what it's giving up and what it refuses to concede.
> 4. **Round 4 — Verdict.** A structured position statement: where the user landed, the strongest argument against it, why they still hold it, confidence level, what would change their mind. "The judgment is yours even though the system staged the fight."
> 
> **Worked example given in the post:** Five sources on "AI and creative work." Standard Perplexity synthesis produced a warm consensus sentence about "human taste" mattering. Cave Test found that two of the sources that seemed *most aligned* were making mutually exclusive definitional claims about what "taste" even means — one defining it as learnable/formalizable/automatable pattern recognition, the other as an emergent, non-computable property of lived experience — with directly opposed practical implications (whether creative advantage has an expiration date or is a permanent moat).
> 
> **Operational caveats given (the "field guide"):**
> - Source selection shapes everything — pre-filtering before running it just reflects the user's own filter back; run on a broad set including skeptical sources.
> - Some flagged conflicts are false — two sources can appear to clash while answering different versions of the question (e.g., enterprise vs. consumer); the system flags, the user judges relevance.
> - Confidence score reflects process (breadth of source set), not conclusion validity — high confidence from a narrow set is worth less than medium confidence from a broad one.
> - The verdict can be wrong if user domain knowledge contradicts it — trust what you know; it means the source set had a gap.
> - **It does not verify facts** — explicitly a different job from fact-checking; it stages sources against each other but doesn't confirm claims against original URLs.
> 
> **Inputs:** A set of sources (or a topic to research) pasted in.
> **Outputs:** A contradiction map with fault lines + stakes, followed by a structured position/verdict statement.
> 
> **What makes it work (load-bearing choices):**
> 1. **The Round 3 forced-steelman step** ("make the other side's best argument before poking holes in it") is the specific mechanism that keeps the system from collapsing back into synthesis — it's an explicit counter-measure against the model's own default behavior.
> 2. **Separating "finds contradictions" from "verifies facts" as two different jobs** — this scope boundary is what lets the tool stay fast/cheap (no need to re-fetch and verify every source) while still being trustworthy for its stated purpose.
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 4. Content Autopsy (from "The million-dollar post-mortem")
> 
> **Name:** The (AI-assisted) Content Autopsy
> 
> **Job:** Extract multi-dimensional diagnostic value from a single failed piece of content, on the premise that a failure reveals more distinct failure points than a win reveals replicable patterns.
> 
> **Mechanics — four steps:**
> 
> **Step 1 — Gather the evidence.** Pick one piece that unequivocally bombed (not "did okay") against a clear stated goal (leads/subscribers/traffic). Collect URL, full text, and analytics.
> 
> **Step 2 — "Three Perspectives" analysis.** Same content run through three distinct AI personas/prompts:
> - **A. SEO Detective (search engine's why).** Prompt: given a target keyword + content text, identify the top 3 things a searcher is likely trying to accomplish for that keyword; judge whether the content fulfills one in the first 200 words ("be brutally honest"); name the biggest mismatch between keyword promise and content payoff.
> - **B. Impatient Audience (reader's why).** Prompt: AI role-plays a skeptical, busy expert-reader with 30 seconds. Reads only headline + first two paragraphs — what promise did they infer? Skims the rest — promise kept or bait-and-switch? Quotes the single most confusing/boring line that triggered exit.
> - **C. The CEO (strategist's why).** Prompt: given audience + stated goal, was this the right FORMAT (blog/webinar/video) and right CHANNEL (Google/LinkedIn/newsletter)? Propose one alternative format/channel for the same core idea.
> 
> **Step 3 — Cross-reference with a winner.** Run the identical three prompts against the creator's best-performing piece. Build a two-column doc/spreadsheet ("Failed Post Analysis" vs "Successful Post Analysis"), paste all six AI responses, and diff them directly (where did the failure miss intent, what made the winner's hook work).
> 
> **Step 4 — Formulate the "Resurrection Hypothesis."** A single causal-diagnosis sentence template, demonstrated:
> &gt; "The original post failed because it was a long, academic blog post that mismatched search intent (The Search Engine's Why) and had a boring intro (The Reader's Why). We believe a short, tactical video script based on the same core idea, distributed on LinkedIn (The Strategist's Why), will succeed."
> 
> **Underlying psychological concept named ("experience-taking"):** Readers approach content with a surface goal (learn X) and a deeper goal (feel like the kind of person who is expert at X). A "Reader's Why" failure is diagnosed as a failure to let the reader "try on" that identity — "you gave them a manual when they wanted to feel like a magician."
> 
> **Framework citation supporting the whole practice:** Matthew Syed's *Black Box Thinking* — the argument that the single greatest predictor of cross-field success is a shame-free system for investigating failure; the autopsy is explicitly framed as "your black box."
> 
> **Inputs:** One failed content piece (URL/text/analytics) + one successful piece for comparison, both run through 3 fixed prompt templates.
> **Outputs:** Six structured AI critiques (3 per piece) + a diffed comparison + one hypothesis sentence naming the specific fix and format/channel change.
> 
> **What makes it work (load-bearing choices):**
> 1. **Fixed three-persona decomposition (SEO/Reader/Strategist)** forces the diagnosis across three independently falsifiable failure surfaces (discovery, hook, distribution strategy) instead of one vague "why didn't this work" prompt — this is what prevents the "shallow one-dimensional analysis" the author says her first attempts produced.
> 2. **Mandatory paired comparison against a winner using the identical prompts** — the diagnosis isn't accepted from the failure analysis alone; the differential against a controlled "winning" run is what turns the autopsy into an actionable, non-generic hypothesis.
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 5. Receptivity Calendar / Content Timing Intelligence System (from "Your content is landing when nobody's home")
> 
> **Name:** No single branded name given in the excerpt; system built across Steps 1-5, centered on the "Receptivity Calendar."
> 
> **Job:** Solves the audience-timing problem distinct from content-quality — mapping WHEN an audience is cognitively capable of receiving content, not just what content is worth their attention. Framed via chronos (clock time) vs. kairos (the right/opportune moment); content is published once but encountered across "a thousand different psychological states" (Borges' Garden of Forking Paths).
> 
> **Mechanics — five-step system:**
> 
> **Step 1 — Build a content taxonomy** (cognitive-state-based, not topic-based; Borges' absurd animal-taxonomy example used to argue all taxonomies are constructed, so pick one that maps to cognitive states). Five dimensions:
> - Cognitive load: Tactical (&lt;30 min, actionable) vs. Strategic (requires thinking time)
> - Intent: Educational / Inspirational / Promotional
> - Funnel stage: Awareness / Consideration / Decision
> - Format: How-to / Framework / Case Study / Opinion
> - Platform: Blog / Substack / LinkedIn / Twitter / Video / Podcast / Other
> Process: export existing content list (title/description/URL/platform), feed to Claude/GPT-4 with a bulk-categorization prompt (full prompt template given), output a structured table for Google Sheets.
> 
> **Step 2 — Analyze performance patterns.** Export publish date/time, engagement metrics, and Step-1 taxonomy tags for every published piece; feed to Claude/GPT-4 with a prompt asking for: days/times of highest &amp; lowest engagement per content-type category, monthly/quarterly patterns, correlation with external timing factors (month/quarter boundaries, industry events, holidays), and platform-specific variation. Worked example: author's own tool's tactical how-tos performed 2.8x better in the first two weeks of each quarter; strategic positioning pieces hit 3.7x baseline in the final three weeks — "execution mode" vs. "planning mode" audience states.
> 
> **Step 2.5 — Validate causality (Popperian falsification gate) before trusting any pattern.** Explicit warning against optimizing on confounded correlations (e.g., higher-quality content happened to also be published at quarter-end). Validation prompt checks each pattern against: content-quality control, topic-relevance control, audience-growth control, external-events control, and sample-size assessment — outputting a **causality rating** per pattern:
> - STRONG SIGNAL (10+ examples) → trust for scheduling
> - EMERGING PATTERN (5-9) → directional guidance only
> - EARLY HYPOTHESIS (2-4) → worth testing, stay flexible
> - INSUFFICIENT EVIDENCE (1-2) → don't act on it
> Named cognitive-bias guardrail: "Noble Cause Corruption" (borrowed from law-enforcement ethics) — teams sacrifice strategic timing because "consistency" feels responsible, publishing mediocre content at a suboptimal moment rather than waiting; "the commitment to consistency becomes more important than the strategy it was meant to serve." Rule of thumb: "Silence during an avoidance zone performs better than content published at the wrong cognitive moment."
> 
> **Step 3 — Build the Receptivity Calendar,** four dimensions per content type:
> 1. **Temporal Windows** — specific day/time patterns above baseline (e.g., "Tuesday 8-10 AM performs 2.3x baseline... Friday afternoon 0.4x baseline").
> 2. **Cyclical Triggers** — recurring external events causing receptivity spikes: quarter boundaries (first 2 weeks = execution mode, last 3 weeks = planning mode), month boundaries (first week = fresh-start energy/budget, last week = reporting mode), industry events (2-3 weeks before = research mode, week of = avoid, week after = implementation-mode spike), economic cycles (tax season, budget season, fiscal year-end).
> 3. **Content-Specific Avoidance Zones** — the inverse map, when NOT to publish a given content type (e.g., tactical how-tos during holiday weeks; promotional content the week before a major industry event; strategic frameworks mid-quarter).
> 4. **Confidence Scoring** — 1-5 scale per pattern based on sample size/consistency (5 = 10+ consistent examples, 3 = 5-9 with variance, 1 = 2-4, needs more data); prevents rigid over-optimization on noisy patterns.
> A full prompt template is given to have AI auto-generate the complete 4-dimension calendar as structured, sheet-pastable text from the validated patterns.
> 
> **Step 4 — Build the smart content schedule (two implementation versions):**
> - **Version A (manual, ~15 min):** 3-tab Google Sheet — Tab 1 Receptivity Calendar (pasted from Step 3), Tab 2 Content Queue (Title/Content Type/Topic/Current Date/Urgency/Status), Tab 3 Scheduling Recommendations (Title/Recommended Date/Recommended Time/Rationale/Performance Multiplier/Override Schedule?). New content added to Tab 2 → its row + prompt pasted to Claude → output pasted to Tab 3.
>   - **Strategic Friction Prompt**: forces AI to ask exactly ONE clarifying question before giving a timing recommendation and wait for the answer (cites Sam Illingworth's "Why AI Answers Too Fast for Us to Think" — instant answers remove the friction that produces real understanding). Full prompt given, asks for: 3 optimal timing windows in next 30 days, expected multiplier + rationale per window, applicable cyclical triggers/avoidance zones, a "good enough" timing option if urgency is High (must not sacrifice &gt;30% performance vs. optimal), and whether the recommendation conflicts with a "regular schedule" habit and why strategic timing should override it.
> - **Version B (automated, ~30-45 min setup, via Make.com free tier):** New row in Tab 2 → Make.com watches for it → sends prompt + content details + receptivity calendar to Claude via OpenRouter API (model: anthropic/claude-3.5-sonnet or 4.5) with a system message locking in the "always ask one clarifying question first" behavior → Claude's question emailed to the user via Gmail module → Make.com pauses for the email reply → reply sent back to Claude → recommendation written to Tab 3 automatically. "Your calendar becomes self-aware."
> 
> **Step 5 — Refinement loop (optional, ~10 min/month).** Pull last 30 days published content, compare actual performance to predicted multipliers, feed both back to Claude/GPT-4 with a prompt asking: which patterns are proving accurate (raise confidence scores), which aren't holding up (lower/remove), any new emerging patterns, any external factors shifting receptivity. Keeps the calendar self-correcting rather than static.
> 
> **Inputs:** Historical content library + performance data (views/clicks/engagement/time-on-page) + publish timestamps.
> **Outputs:** A 4-dimension Receptivity Calendar per content type, a running Scheduling Recommendations sheet, and (Version B) an automated Make.com pipeline that produces per-piece timing recommendations with rationale.
> 
> **What makes it work (load-bearing choices):**
> 1. **The causality-validation gate (Step 2.5) sitting between pattern discovery and calendar-building** — without it, the system would just encode correlation as if it were mechanism; the falsification step (and the four confidence tiers gating what's allowed into the calendar) is what keeps recommendations from being superstition dressed as data.
> 2. **The forced single clarifying question before any timing recommendation** — a deliberate friction injection that prevents the system from defaulting to generic best-practice output and forces it to condition on the specific piece/urgency at hand.</result>

> ## 1. GREENHOUSE (Idea-Tending Agent)
> 
> **Name:** GREENHOUSE (also referenced as "the greenhouse-idea-cultivator" agent package)
> 
> **Job:** Tends half-formed ideas over time — instead of archiving notes in "sealed jars," it tracks whether an idea is growing, cross-references it against other ideas automatically, and tells you the exact moment it's ready to become content (or should be composted).
> 
> **Mechanics — full architecture:**
> 
> *Directory / file structure (mirrors a real garden):*
> ```
> greenhouse-idea-cultivator/
>   CLAUDE.md            # agent identity/persona (name, color, model:opus, memory:project)
>   README.md            # distribution pitch
>   ONBOARDING.md         # user-facing how-to
>   EXAMPLE-SEED.md        # format reference
>   garden/                # SELF-CONTAINED data root — only place writes happen
>     inbox/                # temp holding during external sync
>     seeds/                 # active ideas (growing)
>     ready/                  # ripened, ready to write
>     compost/                 # retired, never deleted
>     garden-state.md          # the agent's "brain" / index, read FIRST every time
>   claude/
>     rules/                 # 11 always-on files, loaded every session
>       00-start.md            # entry point, banner, config check, inline onboarding
>       01-config.md            # STATUS: UNCONFIGURED/CONFIGURED, garden path, idea source
>       02-critical-rules.md    # sandbox constraint: writes ONLY inside garden/
>       03-scope.md             # what agent does/doesn't do (no research, no writing final content, no deletion)
>       04-personality.md       # gardener voice: observant, patient, "observations not directives"
>       05-file-structure.md    # seed file format spec, garden-state.md format spec
>       06-cross-referencing.md # read garden-state.md first, only open seed files as needed
>       07-ripeness-criteria.md # the 5-criteria/3-threshold system
>       08-composting-logic.md  # wilting/orphan/compost rules, convergence detection
>       09-user-patterns.md     # tracks planting frequency, dominant themes, germination engagement, ripeness response
>       98-end-of-session.md    # closing ritual: update state, capture patterns, flag upcoming wilts, update memory
>     skills/                 # 5 on-demand workflows triggered by commands
>       first-time-setup.md     # onboarding wizard (auto-triggers when UNCONFIGURED)
>       greenhouse.md            # dashboard command
>       plant.md                 # add idea (direct or MCP sync)
>       ripen.md                  # ripeness check / promote to ready/
>       compost.md                 # wilting/orphan review
> ```
> 
> *11 rule files group into 4 functions (per the newsletter post itself):*
> 1. **Identity** (3 files): 00-start (entry/first-vs-returning-user detection), 01-config (garden location + note source), 04-personality (voice)
> 2. **Garden mechanics** (3 files): 05-file-structure (spec), 06-cross-referencing (intelligence layer — index-first, targeted-read), 07-ripeness-criteria (5 criteria, 3/5 threshold, auto-move to ready/)
> 3. **Edges** (3 files): 03-scope (what it won't do), 08-composting-logic (wilting=14 days, orphan=10 days, convergence detection), 09-user-patterns (4 tracked behaviors, calibrates agent tone/frequency)
> 4. **Session integrity** (2 files): 02-critical-rules (sandbox — never write outside garden/), 98-end-of-session (closing ritual)
> 
> *Vocabulary (core metaphor mapping):*
> - **Seed** = a thought with no connections yet
> - **Signal** = evidence attached to a seed (another source saying something similar)
> - **Seedling** = just planted, raw, no gravity
> - **Growing** = other ideas pulling toward it
> - **Ripening** = close to writable/actionable
> - **Wilting** = the plant is asking for a decision
> - **Composting** = retiring the idea (never deletion — moved to compost/)
> 
> *Seed file format* (`{ID}-{slug}.md`):
> ```markdown
> # [Seed Title]
> - ID: [number]
> - Status: seedling | growing | ripening | wilting | orphan
> - Planted: YYYY-MM-DD
> - Last activity: YYYY-MM-DD
> - Signals: [count]
> - Connections: [list of connected seed IDs + names, or "none yet"]
> - Ripeness: [X/5 criteria met — list which]
> 
> ## Original signal
> ## Germination
> ## Attached signals
> ### Signal N — [date]
> ## Agent notes
> ## History
> - YYYY-MM-DD: [action taken]
> ```
> 
> *Ripeness criteria — 3 of 5 required to move seeds/ → ready/:*
> 1. **Signal Diversity** — signals from 2+ different source types (article + personal experience, tweet + client conversation, etc.) — "an idea that only comes from one source might be an echo"
> 2. **Cluster Size** — connected to 2+ other seeds
> 3. **Tension Present** — unresolved question/contrarian angle ("an observation without a question is journalism, an observation WITH a question is content"). If germination skipped and tension unclear, flag: "Tension unclear — needs your input."
> 4. **Personal Stake** — user engaged beyond planting (answered germination Qs, added a signal manually, interacted during review, or resisted composting)
> 5. **Age Threshold** — at least 14 days old — "the most powerful principle... patience is a feature"
> 
> *Germination (happens ONLY at planting time, never queued):* two questions —
> - "What made you notice this?" (captures why it caught attention)
> - "Do you agree with it or resist it?" (captures tension/stance)
> These boost Personal Stake and Tension Present criteria. Skippable, no nagging.
> 
> *Composting logic:*
> - **Wilting**: 14 days, no new signals AND no user interaction → status flag, mentioned in "by the way" addendum
> - **Orphan**: no connections to any seed/theme after 10 days
> - **Composting**: files moved to compost/ (never deleted); user always decides (agent never auto-composts); new seeds are cross-referenced against compost — "this connects to something you composted in [month]. Want to revive it?"
> - **Convergence detection**: flagged when 2+ seeds share ≥2 of: same cluster membership, overlapping signal sources, similar framing. Threshold deliberately LOW — false positives treated as useful ("if the user says 'no, these are different, here's why,' that clarification deepens both seeds")
> 
> *Cross-referencing strategy (the "intelligence layer"):* NEVER read all seed files on every interaction.
> 1. Read garden-state.md first (Active Themes, Unattached Seeds, Convergence Warnings)
> 2. Use thematic index to place new input
> 3. Only open specific seed files when going deeper
> 4. Update garden-state.md after processing
> 5. Check compost/ index for revival opportunities
> This is what makes it scale at 10 or 100 seeds without re-reading everything.
> 
> *garden-state.md structure (both brain and dashboard):* Last updated/last sync/next seed ID timestamps; "How to Read This Garden" legend; Vital Stats (active/growing/ripening/wilting/orphans/composted counts); Active Themes (clusters w/ seed IDs+names); Unattached Seeds; Ripeness Tracker (seeds at 2+/5); Convergence Warnings; Orphan Watch; Compost Candidates; User Patterns section.
> 
> *Four user-facing commands (single entry point, deliberately not two):*
> - `Greenhouse` → dashboard (vital stats, themes, ripeness tracker, convergence warnings, orphan watch)
> - `Plant` → dual-mode: direct paste OR sync from Notion/Obsidian via MCP (falls back gracefully to direct if no MCP/sync fails). Link-without-context handling: agent reads URL, summarizes, then asks "what caught YOUR attention about it?" — "a seed without the person's fingerprint is just a bookmark."
> - `Ripen` → shows seeds at 2+ criteria, what's met/missing, specific action to advance each; auto-promotes crossings to ready/
> - `Compost` → presents ALL wilting/orphan candidates with context; user decides per-seed: compost or revive (reset to seedling, restart timers)
> 
> *Design decisions explicitly killed (from the post):*
> - Killed separate `/signal` command — one entry point (`/plant`) forces the agent, not the user, to sort new input into new-seed-vs-signal. "Having two commands put cognitive load back on the user, exactly what the system is supposed to remove."
> - Killed germination queue — germination happens immediately at planting, not batched/deferred.
> - Killed extra abstraction layers (clusters as separate folders, signals as staging area) — "the file system IS the garden." Seeds are literally files; compost is literally a folder; moving a file seeds/→ready/ IS the harvest action.
> 
> *Two-part response pattern (personality rule):* every command returns (1) direct answer to what was asked, (2) "by the way" background addendum (convergence warnings, wilting alerts, new connections) — shortened/less frequent if user ignores them repeatedly (tracked via user-patterns).
> 
> *Critical/sandbox rules:* Never write outside garden/ unless explicitly asked. Can only READ from Obsidian/Notion/local folders via MCP — never write there. No images (text/links only). Always use human-readable seed names in conversation, never bare IDs. No single-seed-view command (users open files directly). No post-harvest command (ready/ is just a folder to open). Every file op followed by a garden-state.md update + History-section append. Nothing ever deleted.
> 
> **Inputs and outputs:**
> - In: raw thoughts, links, observations, quotes, conversation fragments, patterns noticed — with optional germination answers; optional Notion/Obsidian sync via MCP
> - Out: a dashboard (vital stats/themes/ripeness/convergence/orphans), cross-referenced seed files, and a `ready/` folder of ripened ideas the user then writes (agent does NOT write final content)
> 
> **What makes it work (load-bearing choices):**
> 1. **The file system literally IS the garden** — no abstraction between metaphor and mechanism. Folder = state of being; moving a file = an act of judgment. This is why every operation "makes sense without explanation."
> 2. **garden-state.md-first reading discipline** — the agent never opens all seed files; it reads the index, decides where things connect, and only opens specific files when needed. This is what makes the system scale (10 seeds or 100 seeds, same cost).
> 3. Secondary but important: the 14-day Age Threshold criterion is described as "the most powerful principle" — it's an anti-reactive-content mechanism, deliberately preventing premature writing ("Before: you had a great shower thought, wrote it the next day, it felt flat, abandoned it. After: same thought, left to collect signals, 18 days later connects to a client conversation and suddenly the angle is obvious").
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 2. The Pensieve Workflow (brain dumps → content ideas, Dumbledore-style)
> 
> **Name:** Not formally named by Mia in-post (referred to as "your Pensieve" / "the Pensieve workflow"); metaphor borrowed from Harry Potter's memory-extraction device.
> 
> **Job:** Converts a messy, unfiltered weekly brain dump into a strategically prioritized, non-generic content pipeline — solving "I have too many half-formed thoughts and no system to filter them."
> 
> **Mechanics (a linear, timed, prompt-chained pipeline):**
> 
> **Step 1 — Weekly brain dump.** 15 minutes, once/week (she does Thursday afternoons), single clean doc, write without stopping/editing/judging. No filtering, no self-censoring — "the moment you start performing for an imagined audience, you lose the raw material that makes the output unique." Success = 300–500 messy, unfiltered words that would embarrass you if anyone read them.
> 
> **Step 2 — Extract strategic themes.** Copy the full brain dump into an AI assistant with this exact prompt structure:
> - Persona: "qualitative researcher specializing in thematic analysis and strategic positioning"
> - LAYER 1: Surface Themes — identify 3-5 explicit topics
> - LAYER 2: Underlying Tensions — for each theme, identify the tension/contradiction
> - LAYER 3: Strategic Implications — for each tension: why it matters to audience (not just you), what unique angle it gives you, what blind spot it reveals about the market
> - Output format: Theme / Tension / Strategic Opportunity / Competitive Blind Spot
> Time: 5 min run + 2 min read. Success = 3-5 themes with strategic implications, not just topic summaries.
> 
> **Step 2.5 — Business impact scoring.** Take the 3-5 themes and run a second prompt: persona = "business strategist," provide themes + business context (product, ICP, current challenges, revenue goal), score each theme 1-10 on: (1) Strategic Alignment to ICP pain points, (2) Competitive Differentiation (unique vs. competitors), (3) Revenue Proximity (how directly connects to signups/trials/inquiries). Output: ranked list + recommendation of which theme to prioritize THIS WEEK. Time: 3 min run + 2 min review.
> 
> **[Aside: Premortem framing]** — borrows Luca Dellanna's "Premortem" mental model (assume the project already failed, work backward) to justify Steps 3-4: the single biggest failure mode for a content strategy fed by AI brain dumps is boring, undifferentiated ideas ("your ideas were boring... you didn't have a point of view, so you tried to copy everyone else's").
> 
> **Step 3 — The Boring Detector.** Pick the highest-scoring theme. Prompt AI (persona: "expert content strategist with a brutal intolerance for generic ideas") to generate 15 raw content angles, then run EACH through 5 pass/fail tests:
> 1. **Copycat Test** — could a competitor with ChatGPT write this exact angle in 30 seconds? If YES → reject.
> 2. **Google Test** — does a version already rank top-10 for related keywords? If YES → reject.
> 3. **POV Test** — does this require MY specific context/experience/perspective? If NO → reject.
> 4. **Controversy Test** — would ≥20% of the audience disagree or feel uncomfortable? If NO → reject.
> 5. **Specificity Test** — can you name a specific person/company/moment exemplifying this angle? If NO → reject.
> Process: generate 15 raw angles → run through all 5 tests → present only the ones passing ≥4/5 → for each survivor show which tests passed and a 1-sentence "why it's not boring" justification. If fewer than 5 angles survive, the theme itself isn't differentiated enough — go back to Step 2.5's #2 theme. Time: 5 min run + 3 min review. Success = 8-10 sharp, debatable, POV-specific angles.
> 
> **Step 4 — Title/subtitle generation.** Take the winning angle, prompt for 10 title+subtitle pairs with rules: no generic clickbait, strong active verbs, subtitle must clarify or add a compelling twist, at least 3 titles must include a specific number or named tactic.
> 
> **Bonus Step 0 — Monthly Meta-Pensieve.** Once a month (last Friday), collect the last 4 weekly brain dumps and run a prompt (persona: "strategic analyst") to find DEEPER cross-week patterns: (1) The Meta-Question — the one question kept being asked in different forms; (2) The Philosophical Tension — the underlying tension driving multiple themes; (3) The Content Thesis — what the next quarter of content should be ABOUT at the highest level. Explicitly framed as "not about summarizing... about finding the signal I'm too close to see."
> 
> **Critical implementation note:** the system only works if Step 1's integrity is protected — brain dumps must be truly unfiltered; performing for an imagined audience destroys the raw material.
> 
> **Inputs and outputs:**
> - In: 15-minute unfiltered weekly brain dump (300-500 words); optionally 4 weeks of dumps for the monthly meta-pass
> - Out: a ranked, business-scored theme; a set of 8-10 boring-detector-passed content angles; 10 title/subtitle pairs for the chosen angle; monthly, a "content thesis" statement
> 
> **What makes it work:**
> 1. **Layered AI prompting mimics Dumbledore's Pensieve function** — the AI is used purely as an external pattern-recognition processor over material the human already generated raw, not as an idea generator from scratch. The chain (surface themes → tension → strategic implication → business scoring → boring-filter → naming) converts vague feelings into named, testable, prioritized artifacts at each step.
> 2. **The Boring Detector's reject-by-default framing** — every angle must actively survive 5 explicit falsifiable tests before being shown to the user, which structurally prevents the "AI thinks all your ideas are good ideas" failure mode she names as the core problem the whole system solves.
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 3. The Morning Light Framework (pushing an idea past "interesting")
> 
> **Name:** The Morning Light (also called "the Morning Light Framework")
> 
> **Job:** A second-stage filter for ideas that already ripened/survived initial elimination — tests whether a "ready" or "exciting" idea is actually worth committing real creative energy to, separating genuine tension/value from mere novelty-driven dopamine.
> 
> **Premise/psychology behind it:** AI-accelerated ideation floods you with novelty (dopamine/"wanting") faster than your evaluation system ("liking," per Kent Berridge's research) can keep up — every idea feels like "the one" in the moment because the brain is reacting to newness, not quality. The fix, inspired by Monet repainting the same water lilies at different hours ("morning revealed structure that afternoon concealed"): never commit to an idea in the moment it excites you — always return to it after time has passed and the dopamine has cleared, and test it structurally.
> 
> **Mechanics — 4-question framework, each with a definition, criteria, and a copy-paste AI prompt:**
> 
> **Question 1 — Is there tension, or just novelty?**
> Tension = unresolved friction (two legitimate sides, real disagreement possible). Novelty = mere newness, fades in ~20 minutes.
> Traits of real tension: (a) two legitimate sides exist — you can argue the opposite convincingly; (b) friction must be felt — audience immediately recognizes it from their own work; (c) specificity makes it stronger — narrowing the conflict should make it MORE interesting, not less; (d) refuses easy resolution — can't be solved in one sentence; (e) touches their Monday — must connect to something in the audience's actual daily work, not a far-off abstraction.
> Prompt: paste idea + intended format, ask AI to identify the two sides, flag if it's actually just an observation (no real friction), test narrowing, test one-sentence resolvability, and locate where it shows up in the audience's daily work.
> 
> **Question 2 — Does this idea have a clear enemy?**
> The "enemy" is a behavior/belief/practice, not a person. A strong enemy: (a) can be named specifically (vague enemies → vague work); (b) has genuine frustration behind it (audience should already be nodding); (c) has defenders (if nobody would defend the thing being attacked, it's not a real enemy — it's a strawman); (d) forces a behavior change (naming the enemy must imply a better alternative, not just complaint).
> Prompt: asks AI to name what the idea is fighting (as behavior/belief/practice), who defends it, show a concrete example of what the enemy produces, and distinguish "existing pain" from "manufactured pain" (naming real pain = powerful; inventing pain = manipulation).
> 
> **Question 3 — Can someone do something with this?** (Activation, not agreement)
> "'Interesting' is the most dangerous compliment your work can get" — it means someone appreciated it and went back to their day unchanged. Target = activation: someone does something tangible within 24 hours. Markers: they can start TODAY; the required action is hyper-specific; the payoff is fast; the action hijacks their existing workflow (doesn't require setting up something new); the mechanism has zero friction.
> Prompt: asks AI to specify exactly what someone does Monday morning (not "think about X differently" but the literal first step), whether they can start within 24 hours without buying/learning/setting up anything new, how fast the payoff arrives, and whether it plugs into existing work or demands something new.
> 
> **Question 4 — What does this look like in their life, not yours?** (Transferability)
> An idea can pass tension/enemy/activation and still only matter to its author if it's trapped in their specific context/workflow/bubble. Transferability = "does this idea travel?"
> Four markers: (a) crosses industry lines — a professor, consultant, PM, and newsletter writer should all recognize the core problem without translation; (b) survives without your exact tech stack — holds whether someone uses Claude, ChatGPT, Notion, Figma, or a future tool; (c) payoff lands on human experience, not output; (d) the story works like a mirror (personal detail must point past itself).
> Prompt: imagine 3 named audience segments — does the idea matter to all three? Strip away your specific context — does the core principle still hold? Would someone in a different field recognize the problem without your explaining it? Does the example illuminate a broader principle or only itself?
> 
> **Combined/master prompt (run all 4 in sequence against a ripened seed):**
> &gt; "A seed ripened in my GREENHOUSE agent. Before I commit to building on this, I want to run it through the Morning Light. Here's the seed file: [paste]. Run all four Morning Light questions in sequence... For each question, give me your honest assessment with specific evidence from the seed file. Does it pass or not? If it doesn't pass, tell me what's missing and whether it's fixable. After all four, give me the verdict: commit, revise, or return to the GREENHOUSE for more tending."
> 
> **How it connects to GREENHOUSE explicitly (this is the pipeline seam):** GREENHOUSE's 3/5 ripeness threshold tells you an idea is "ready" (tended, cross-referenced, connected). The Morning Light tests whether "ready" also means "worth committing to" — "A seed can ripen in a greenhouse and still collapse under the Morning Light because it lacks tension or has no enemy." The stated 4-move complete pipeline: (1) Capture freely (brain dumps → GREENHOUSE), (2) Tend patiently (let the agent do its job), (3) Test ruthlessly (ripened seed → Morning Light), (4) Commit fully (build only what survives).
> 
> Not all four questions are needed every time — e.g. a LinkedIn post might only need tension + activation; a product feature might only need enemy + transferability. "Pick what fits."
> 
> Deliberately NOT built as a pre-packaged Claude Skill: "the Morning Light only works when the questions feel like yours. Pre-built means pre-biased." Recommended usage: run the prompts as-is a few times, swap in your own words, THEN ask Claude to build a personal skill running them all in one go.
> 
> **Inputs and outputs:**
> - In: a single idea (from GREENHOUSE's ready/ folder or elsewhere) + intended format (post/product/course/talk/strategy)
> - Out: a pass/fail verdict per question with evidence, and one of three final verdicts: commit / revise / return to GREENHOUSE for more tending
> 
> **What makes it work:**
> 1. **Deliberate temporal separation from the excitement itself** — running the test the next day (or at least after the initial dopamine response fades) is the core mechanism, not the four questions in isolation. "Just don't let it make your commitments for you. Let the morning do that."
> 2. **Falsifiability by design** — each question is built to actively try to kill the idea ("Be honest. I'd rather kill this now than discover it's flat after I've invested real time building it" / "If the enemy is weak or generic, tell me. I want a fight worth having") rather than validate it, mirroring the Boring Detector's reject-by-default posture from the Pensieve piece.
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 4. The Smuggler Agent / Holiday Memories Content Engine
> 
> **Name:** "Smuggler Agent" (the persona/prompt); the overall workflow is unnamed but described as building "a proprietary content engine using your holiday memories."
> 
> **Job:** Converts non-work personal memories/sensory experiences (holidays, observations) into differentiated, hard-to-copy strategic content/product/distribution ideas — solving the problem that competitors converge on the same generic industry patterns because they draw from the same research/best-practices.
> 
> **Mechanics — 4-step layered pipeline (Layer 1/2/3 progressively more strategic), plus a "60-second Smuggler Challenge" teaser version:**
> 
> **60-second version (single-prompt teaser):** Paste one specific, weird, non-business thing you saw on holiday. Prompt: persona "Smuggler Agent and Strategic Architect," find the "Signal" competitors are missing, produce 3 "Blue Ocean" positioning angles: (1) The Counter-Narrative (what standard industry belief does this memory disprove?), (2) The Aesthetic Moat (how does this memory's "vibe" make your brand voice un-copyable?), (3) The High-Status Shift (how does this specific detail reposition you as an Architect rather than a Grunt?).
> 
> **Full workflow — Step 1: Gather the chaos (&lt;10 min).** Capture raw stimuli, not polished reflections — "think tiny, specific things." Explicit warning: "Do not let the AI hallucinate your inputs. Stop now and write down 5 specific, messy things you saw this week. If you skip this, your results will be generic trash." Build a list of ~20 short bullets from photo roll/memory. Prompt: normalize the raw list into a markdown table with columns — id (numbered), short label (3-5 words), 1-2 sentence sensory description, context type (concert/architecture/street/food/interaction). Return ONLY the table.
> 
> **Step 2: Dissect the feeling (Layer 1: Sensory Deconstruction).** For each row in the table, extract: dominant sense(s), perceived pace (sudden/slow-burn/stop-start/rhythmic/static), contrast (what the moment is contrasted against), tension (what creates uncertainty/discomfort/anticipation), relief/resolution (what creates closure/calm/clarity), abundance-vs-scarcity signals, dominant observer emotion. "Be honest about negative emotions. Sadness is often a better smuggler than Joy." Note embedded in the post: "The AI identified that the value wasn't the music itself, but the signal that effort was about to happen" — i.e., the mechanism-extraction is meant to surface a reusable business insight (e.g., "show the effort/preparation before delivering the polished answer").
> 
> **Step 3: Extract the principle (Layer 2: Strategic Principle Translation).** Given the deconstructed table, infer 1-2 general, reusable strategic principles per row applicable to marketing/product/CX (e.g., "Delayed resolution builds anticipation," "Sensory overload followed by simplicity creates relief and trust," "Clear thresholds signal that a new chapter has started"). Format as table: id, short label, principle 1 (name + explanation), optional principle 2. Explicit instruction: avoid generic clichés; ground each principle in the specifics of the moment.
> 
> **Step 4: Make it move (Layer 3: Applied Experiments).** For each principle, generate 3 micro-experiments, each shippable in 1-3 days with a measurable target: (1) a **Content** experiment (e.g., newsletter segment, landing page variant, social thread), (2) a **Product/offer** experiment (e.g., feature tweak, packaging change, onboarding flow adjustment), (3) a **Distribution/timing** experiment (e.g., publish channel/timing change). Each must clearly embody the principle and include a measurable outcome (example given: "Glitch Header" — combine two clashing aesthetics in a newsletter header, measured by CTR vs. baseline; "Messy Checkout" — replace clean success-page UI with a handwritten "post-it" style graphic, measured by screenshot/share rate; "Wrong Channel Drop" — publish a text-heavy strategy piece on a visual-only platform, measured by engagement/surprise).
> 
> **Two closing refinement tricks:**
> 1. **"Let Joy narrow the scope"** — since the workflow outputs experiments across product/content/distribution indiscriminately, ask AI to focus on just the one category actually relevant to you.
> 2. **"Let Sadness sharpen the stakes"** — explicitly ask "Where did this memory carry loss, fear, disappointment, or longing?" because those are often the places the audience is secretly stuck too. Provided follow-up prompt: "Please give me some ideas on how to implement some of these experiences into my newsletter content pieces (written only). My topic is X, my niche is Y."
> 
> **Inputs and outputs:**
> - In: raw personal/sensory memories (photos, journal entries) — explicitly non-business, non-analytics-derived
> - Out: a table of sensory-deconstructed moments → a table of abstracted strategic principles → a set of 3 concrete, ship-in-1-3-days experiments per principle (content/product/distribution) with success metrics
> 
> **What makes it work:**
> 1. **The "smuggling" framing itself** — treating the AI as a literal border-crossing mechanism between the "raw humanity" domain (memory, sensation) and the "business" domain (strategy, product), which reframes personal experience as usable competitive material rather than irrelevant to work.
> 2. **Progressive abstraction with each layer explicitly separated (sensory facts → mechanism → principle → shippable experiment)** — each step is a distinct, separately-prompted AI call rather than one big ask, which is what lets a raw un-strategic memory ("a violinist takes an audible breath before his solo") survive translation into something testable ("show the effort behind the polish, not just the polished result").
> 
> ---
> 
> ## 5. The Analogy-Finding Process (things that follow you around)
> 
> **Name:** Not formally named as a system by Mia (no capitalized brand name in this post, unlike the others). Sometimes referred to inline as building a "Mechanism Card" / "Analogy Brief" process.
> 
> **Job:** A repeatable method for converting persistent, non-work fascinations (films, books, random facts, memories) into genuinely load-bearing analogies that change how you think, decide, or build — as opposed to analogies that just add charm/decoration to already-finished thinking. Solves the "transfer problem": most curious people can go from "I love this" to appreciating a fact, but rarely reliably convert it into new thinking or new builds.
> 
> **Origin/psychology:** Mia's film-school training ("why is that camera up close? why so high up? what genre? what's in the frame and why?") became a permanent habit of noticing construction beneath surface — later applied to noticing the construction beneath business/AI problems too. Key line: "The useful question to ask yourself is not when you first noticed something but whether you preserve those observations long enough to discover what else they know."
> 
> Explicitly framed as underlying BOTH other systems in the corpus: "My GREENHOUSE agent... I started thinking of ideas as seeds; observations became signals; the connections between them created growth. When I thought about GREENHOUSE this way, the system itself turned into the garden. Remove the plant logic and GREENHOUSE becomes a different agent." — i.e., GREENHOUSE's entire metaphor IS a worked example of this analogy process. Also cites using Herzog's *Fata Morgana* documentary imagery to reframe an internal AI-honesty debate ("what changed because of that pushback?" rather than just "did it push back?").
> 
> **Mechanics — an 8-step process, each with defined inputs/AI prompts, ending in two persistent artifact types (Mechanism Card, Analogy Brief):**
> 
> **1. Begin with something real.** Write down what caught your attention, why it stuck, what it made you wonder — first response stays entirely the user's own (not the AI's). Then ask AI to study the material: "I want to study this fact before deciding if it's useful for my work. Help me determine the facts accurately and separate what is known from what I might be assuming. Ask me enough questions about the part that caught my attention most. Don't suggest ideas or analogies just yet." (AI explicitly forbidden from jumping to analogy generation at this stage.)
> 
> **2. Work out what's happening underneath it.** Prompt AI to give several possible mechanisms (forces/constraints/tensions/sequences/transformations) explaining the fact — WITHOUT the AI deciding which is correct. For each, explain the conditions that make it true and where the explanation stops holding. User then picks the one that feels accurate/alive/complete.
> 
> **3. Preserve it before forcing a use.** Store as a "Mechanism Card" (even if nothing useful follows immediately — "just leave the mechanism card right there"):
> ```
> SOURCE:
> WHAT CAUGHT MY ATTENTION:
> FACTS TO KEEP STRAIGHT:
> THE MECHANISM UNDERNEATH IT:
> CONDITIONS THAT MAKE IT TRUE:
> WHERE IT MAY TRAVEL:
> WHERE IT BREAKS:
> OPEN QUESTIONS:
> ```
> 
> **4. Connect from either direction.** Two entry paths:
> - If the observation comes first: ask AI to compare the mechanism card against current projects/problems/unfinished ideas, looking for structural similarity (not word similarity), and to show where each connection breaks.
> - If the problem comes first: give AI the live problem + saved mechanism cards, ask it to "look for the same arrangement of forces rather than similar words or subjects. Show me only the connections you can explain structurally, and tell me where each one may break."
> 
> **5. Run the Load-Bearing test.** For the most promising candidate connections, answer:
> - "What becomes visible in my work now that I noticed this?"
> - "What does this change about my questions or decisions or methods?"
> - "Can the analogy be removed without changing the idea the connection reveals?"
> Optionally ask AI to try to disprove the analogy — identify vocabulary that only "scratches the surface" rather than a real shared mechanism, and point out ignored differences.
> 
> **6. Decide what the connection should become.** If it survives the load-bearing test, decide its job: help you think differently, change a decision/system, or explain a hard idea to someone else. Ask AI to translate it into the needed form — argument / decision / method / workflow / product mechanism / explanation / visual direction — with explicit instruction: "Preserve the mechanism and its limits. Do not add new claims or make the analogy carry more than we've established."
> 
> **7. Save the result — "Analogy Brief":**
> ```
> LIVE PROBLEM OR IDEA:
> SOURCE MATERIAL:
> SHARED MECHANISM:
> WHAT THE CONNECTION REVEALS:
> WHAT IT CHANGES:
> WHERE IT BREAKS:
> FORM IT SHOULD TAKE:
> NEXT DECISION OR ACTION:
> ```
> 
> **8. Repeat before you automate.** One useful analogy proves nothing about your process — you need several examples before pattern-mining your own habit. Then ask AI: "Study these examples and identify repeated patterns in how I make useful connections. Look at the source material I notice, the questions I ask, the mechanisms I recognise, why I accept or reject a connection, the jobs I ask an analogy to do, and which decisions remain mine. Separate recurring patterns from one-off choices. Do not invent a coherent process where the evidence is thin." User then corrects errors and uses the corrected record as the actual basis of their process (rather than pre-building a generic skill upfront).
> 
> **9. Decide whether this needs a permanent system.** Only after confirmed, repeated patterns exist, optionally turn it into a custom Skill, a dedicated agent, or (explicitly) a new mode inside GREENHOUSE — "your observations and ideas will all live there and the GREENHOUSE agent already has the capabilities to read, write, and edit files." Explicit division of labor if automated: AI does research/retrieval/comparison/questions/documentation/testing/preserving/translating-back-into-fields; AI is explicitly FORBIDDEN from deciding what catches your attention, inventing personal meaning, deciding which connections to approve, concealing where an analogy breaks, or deciding what the work should become.
> 
> **Named 8-verb summary of the whole pipeline:** "Notice, understand, preserve, retrieve, connect, load-test, translate, observe your pattern, encode."
> 
> **Two categories of analogy value distinguished in the post:**
> - Analogies that help you **think differently** (dragonfly compound-eyes → questioning AI's single-lens "can I automate this" decision-making default)
> - Analogies that help you **build differently** (plant-growth logic → the entire GREENHOUSE architecture)
> - Analogies that help you **explain differently** (Herzog's *Fata Morgana* → reframing what "useful pushback from AI" should mean)
> 
> **Inputs and outputs:**
> - In: any persistent, un-asked-for fascination (a film image, a random biological fact, an old memory, an object) — explicitly NOT starting from "what content/product could this become"
> - Out: a Mechanism Card (preserved raw observation + candidate mechanisms), and where the connection survives testing, an Analogy Brief (translated into argument/decision/method/workflow/product-mechanism/explanation/visual-direction) plus a "next decision or action"
> 
> **What makes it work (load-bearing choices):**
> 1. **Mandatory temporal/procedural separation of "what caught your attention" from "what it means for my work"** — the user's raw reaction is captured and preserved as data BEFORE any use is forced onto it (mirrors GREENHOUSE's germination and the Pensieve's unfiltered-brain-dump rule — a recurring pattern across Mia's whole system).
> 2. **The Load-Bearing Test as an explicit falsification gate** ("can the analogy be removed without changing the idea the connection reveals?") — distinguishes decorative analogy (removable, cosmetic) from structural analogy (load-bearing, actually changes what you build/decide), and the process refuses to let AI decide this on the user's behalf.
> 
> ---
> 
> ### Cross-cutting patterns worth flagging for synthesis
> 
> - **Every one of the five systems enforces a mandatory raw-capture-before-interpretation step** and explicitly instructs the AI NOT to interpret/suggest at that stage (germination questions in GREENHOUSE; the "don't filter, don't edit" brain dump in the Pensieve; "don't suggest ideas or analogies just yet" in the analogy process; "don't let AI hallucinate your inputs" in the Smuggler workflow).
> - **Every system builds in an explicit falsification/rejection gate** rather than a generation-only step: Ripeness criteria (3/5 threshold) + Composting in GREENHOUSE; the Boring Detector's 5 reject-by-default tests in the Pensieve; the Morning Light's 4 questions explicitly designed to try to kill the idea; the Load-Bearing Test's "can this be removed without changing anything" in the analogy process.
> - **All systems persist state as human-readable markdown files/tables with fixed field schemas** (seed files, Mechanism Cards, Analogy Briefs, sensory-deconstruction tables) rather than opaque state — consistent with "the file system IS the garden" as a broader design philosophy across her whole corpus.
> - **GREENHOUSE is explicitly positioned by Mia as the connective infrastructure** — the Pensieve/Smuggler/analogy workflows are described as things that could ultimately live inside or feed into GREENHOUSE (e.g., "you can ask AI to integrate it into GREENHOUSE... try it out if you haven't already"), and the Morning Light is explicitly framed as GREENHOUSE's necessary second-stage filter ("ready" ≠ "worth committing to").
>
